- **J2 held:** the idle-power crossover is **83.4 L/min** (all three readings 83 to 93).
- **J3 held:** at the stock 219 L/min pump open-centre idles at **3.56 times** load-sensing's standby (PVG reading).
- **J4 refuted, in the other direction:** at S = 0.38 a smaller fixed pump beats 3.99 L/h in **74.0%** of geometries, up from 66.7%, not down below 50% (5.06 L/h: 47.9%, from 44.3%). The median f_t,max rises from 4.63 to **4.79 L/h**. Over S 0.20 to 0.50 the share is 69.5 to 78.4%.
- **J5 held:** at S = 0.38 the central geometry's best fixed pump falls from 101 to **96 L/min**, and its f_t,max from 5.635 to **5.07 L/h** (S 0.50: 4.84 at 93 L/min).
- **J6 held:** under the Salami text reading the median f_t,max at S = 0.38 is **5.32 L/h** (6i 4.63), and a fixed pump beats 3.99 L/h in 88.0% of geometries.
- Why J4 went the other way: the central geometry is again unrepresentative. Its best fixed pump (101 L/min) is above the 83 L/min crossover, so idle charges it; but over the 384 geometries the best fixed pump is a median **75 L/min**, below the crossover, so idle hours credit it (a small fixed pump circulating 75 L/min through a neutral at about 12.5 bar costs less than a 219 L/min piston pump spinning at zero stroke). No geometry that wins on the record alone stops winning at any S up to 0.95.
- Across all 18 settings (3 neutral readings x standby flow 0, 2, 5 L/min x idle pump speed 1.0, 0.5) at S = 0.38 the share above 3.99 L/h is 71.4% to 91.1% and the median f_t,max 4.73 to 5.64 L/h; idle never lowers the share below 6i's 66.7%. The stock pump: open-centre burns more pump fuel in every geometry, by 4.08 L/h or more per hour of load-sensing record at S = 0.38 (3.12 on the record alone).
- Reading: idle time does not rescue load-sensing on this machine. It hurts the large fixed pumps (the stock one most, as 2i found on the force model) and helps the small ones, because a load-sensing piston pump spinning at zero stroke still drags about 3 kW at the XE80E's size. At the telematics share of 38%, a smaller fixed pump beats the tractor-standard no-load rate in about three quarters of geometries. The decisive unknown is still the one 6g and 6i named: the fixed pump's own full-stroke efficiency, and now its drag too, since a gear pump's spin loss is not a piston pump's.
- Limits: the idle share is trade-press reporting of manufacturer telematics (Komatsu, Volvo CE), not a study of this machine; at idle both pumps' losses come from 6h's fit at 1500 r/min and pressures below the table's 25 bar, scaled by displacement, and the fixed pump is the same piston unit (a gear pump's drag is unsourced); the neutral readings are other valves' curves (PVG 32, Salami VD8A at 7 sections), and the Salami Q^2 law is carried well past its catalogue flows at large pumps; the idle engine speed is not known (1.0 and 0.5 as brackets, drag in bar of full-stroke flow held, an assumption); idle fuel uses the working marginal slope b; a real excavator at idle may hold a lever against a stop, which this charges as neutral.
- Next: (a) unchanged and now carries more weight, a fixed gear pump's full-stroke efficiency and spin loss at the class's size (datasheet or ISO 4409 test); (b) the XE80E's auto-idle speed and an excavator valve's neutral curve; (c) the paper's sections 3.7j and 3.7k (6i and 6j) and the abstract.

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 6i: 6g rerun with the pump's loss continued below the smallest measured stroke, and g measured on the motion trace (predictions written before the motion cache was refilled or any case computed)
- Why: 6h showed 78% of the measured piston pump's loss at 150 bar does not shrink with stroke, so 6g's flat hold below 12 of 45 cm3/r (x0 0.267) flattered load-sensing by at least 10.36 g L/h on the central break-even, with g = E[(1 - x/x0)+] over moving time. g needed the motion trace; huggingface.co was unreachable in 6h. It answers in this session (HTTP 200, probed first), so 6h's Next (a) can run.
- Method fixed now: below x0 the loss keeps its measured value at x0 and falls only by the fitted stroke-proportional term: L(x, p) = L_meas(x0, p) - kc p (x0 - x), L_meas(x0, p) = x0 p (1/eta_table(x0, p) - 1), kc from 6h's full-range fit at each temperature (anchored continuation: continuous with the table at x0, so the measured 12 cm3/r point is used rather than the model's 0.02-off reading of it). At and above x0 eta is 6g's table, unchanged. Load-sensing shaft energy per sample is w (q p_out + L(x, p_out) Q_max) (bar x L/min), so a sample at zero stroke still pays its drag; eta_LS = sum(q w) p_out over that. Everything else as 6g (384 geometries, fixed pump at full stroke from the table, m* = 0.78 (r - 1) p, b 0.2203, 60 C central, 25 C and the 36-set sweep as sensitivities). Also reported: g and s (time share below x0) per geometry; the 6h closed-form bound at the central geometry against the rerun's actual rise; and a pure-model continuation (6h's 4-term fit everywhere below x0) and the post-hoc small-stroke A (23.7 bar) as an upper case.
- **Predictions:**
  - I1: at the central geometry g lies in 0.05 to 0.20 (60 C). Prior 60%.
  - I2: the central f_t,max rises by at least 1.0 L/h over 6g's 5.50. Prior 55%.
  - I3: the share of the 384 geometries where a smaller fixed pump beats 6f's lowest 2100 rpm no-load rate (3.99 L/h) rises from 32.8% to at least 45%. Prior 50%.
  - I4: the central rise is at least 6h's bound 10.36 g evaluated at the measured g, and within 20% of it. Prior 70%.
  - I5: at the stock 219 L/min pump open-centre still burns more pump fuel in every geometry. Prior 90%.
  - I6: the fixed-pump efficiency at which load-sensing wins back at 3.99 L/h (6g: 0.962 of the curve's full-stroke value at the central geometry) falls to 0.95 or below. Prior 55%.

### Outcome of 6i (`experiments/excavator_loss_below_x0.py`, `experiments/excavator_loss_below_x0_results.txt`, 38 s with numpy and h5py; `measured.continued_loss`, `measured.stroke_shortfall`, `fit_pump_loss(max_fraction=)`, 1 new test, 63 in all)
- Before anything new ran, 6g's script was rerun to refill the motion cache from huggingface.co; its output equals the committed file byte for byte. The new script's `flat` mode reproduces 6g (central f_t,max 5.495 L/h, eta_LS 0.76941 on the loss grid and by 6g's exact per-sample loop, 32.8% and 17.2% of geometries).
- Two fixes before the reported run, both found by that check: (1) the first energy formula charged demand above the 219 L/min pump only Q_max L(1), where 6g charged it on q; aligned to 6g (loss times max(1, q/Q_max)), which moved the flat check from 5.456 to 5.495. (2) The central outlet is 149.6 bar, not 150 (P_C = 129.6 from 6b's share of the top pressure), so 6h's A at this outlet is 18.49 bar, not 18.55.
- Below x0 at 149.6 bar and 60 C the anchored loss gives 0.290 at x 0.05, 0.446 at 0.10, 0.610 at 0.20 (6h's model 0.285, 0.440, 0.605; flat 0.672); its zero-stroke loss A_eff = 19.49 - 1.39 = 18.09 bar.
- **I1 refuted:** g at the central geometry is **0.014** (s, the time share below x0, 0.066), below the 0.05 to 0.20 predicted. The central geometry seldom runs the pump below 0.27 of stroke. Over the 384 geometries g is a median **0.129** (p10 0.044, p90 0.311, max 0.446) and s a median 0.446 (0.03 to 0.91): the central geometry is near the bottom of the range.
- **I2 refuted:** the central f_t,max rises only **0.140 L/h**, to 5.635 (model 5.650, post-hoc small-stroke fit 5.732), not by 1 L/h.
- **I3 held, strongly:** a smaller fixed pump beats 6f's lowest 2100 rpm no-load rate (3.99 L/h) in **66.7%** of geometries (6g: 32.8%), and its highest (5.06 L/h) in **44.3%** (6g: 17.2%). The median f_t,max goes from 3.32 to **4.63 L/h** (p90 5.84 to 8.69). Across the continuation rules the 3.99 L/h share is 66.7% (anchored), 70.3% (model) and 88.8% (small-stroke fit, post hoc); at 25 C 77.6%. The rise over flat is a median 1.11 L/h (0.07 to 10.9) and tracks g (correlation 0.82).
- **I4 refuted, narrowly:** the rise at 6g's maximising pump is 0.140 L/h against 6h's bound 10.36 x 0.0142 = 0.147, 0.951 of it. Not a failure of the formula: the anchored continuation uses the measured loss at x0, whose implied A_eff (18.09 bar) sits 2% under the 4-term model's A at this outlet (18.49), and 6h's 10.36 was computed at 150 bar. 6h's "lower bound" holds for the model continuation (rise 0.154 >= 0.147), not for every continuation through the measured point.
- **I5 held:** at the stock 219 L/min pump open-centre still burns more pump fuel in every geometry (minimum gap 3.08 L/h of work, against 3.09 in 6g).
- **I6 refuted:** the fixed-pump efficiency at which load-sensing wins back at 3.99 L/h is 0.959 of the curve at the central geometry (0.806 instead of 0.840; 6g 0.962), not 0.95 or below. Over the 256 geometries where a fixed pump now wins it is a median **0.950** (6g: 0.968 over 126), as low as 0.821.
- Sweep (anchored, 36 sets of margin, pressure and b): at 3.99 L/h a fixed pump wins under every set in 25.5% of geometries (6g 6.2%) and under at least one in 100% (6g 84.1%).
- Reading: the flat hold mattered little where 6g and 6h looked (the central geometry, g 0.014) and a lot everywhere else. Most cylinder-geometry readings of the excavator put the load-sensing pump below 0.27 of stroke for about half its moving time, and with the pump's stroke-independent drag charged there, a smaller fixed pump now beats the tractor-standard no-load rate in two thirds of geometries rather than one third. The fragility found in 6g remains and is now the main question: in those geometries a fixed pump 5% less efficient than this piston pump at full stroke (median), hands the ranking back. The excavator's pump choice turns on the fixed pump's full-stroke efficiency (6g's Next (b)), which is still unsourced.
- Limits: one digitised pump scaled to the XE80E by displacement fraction and to 2100 r/min not at all; below 12 cm3/r the curve is a continuation of a fit, not a measurement (three rules reported, 66.7 to 88.8%); load-sensing's standby flow and compensator still uncharged (they would add to load-sensing's loss, in the same direction); a zero-stroke sample is charged its full drag, though a real machine at standby would see the pump's own standby pressure, not the outlet's 149.6 bar.
- Next: (a) unchanged and now decisive, a full-stroke efficiency for a fixed gear or bent-axis pump of the class at about 150 bar (datasheet or ISO 4409 test); (b) charge load-sensing's standby (pump at standby pressure, not outlet pressure, when no function moves); (c) the paper's section 3.7j and the abstract now carry the reopened share.

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 6h: a physical loss model fitted to Kauranne's curves, tested by predicting the 12 cm3/r curve from the larger strokes, and a closed-form bound on how much 6g's flat hold below 0.27 of stroke flattered load-sensing (predictions written before any fit ran)
- Why: 6g held the pump's efficiency flat below the smallest measured stroke (12 of 45 cm3/r, x0 = 0.267), and said so: "this flatters load-sensing". Holding eta flat means the pump's loss shrinks in proportion to flow as the stroke goes to zero, while a real axial piston pump keeps a loss that does not scale with stroke (barrel churning, slipper and valve-plate leakage and friction under pressure). 6g's Next asked for a full-stroke gear-pump efficiency and a part-stroke curve at about 100 cm3/r. Neither can be sourced this session: every data and publisher host tried (mdpi.com, tuprints, iris.unimore.it, manufacturer sites, doi.org, huggingface.co) is outside the session's network allowance; only GitHub and package registries answer. huggingface.co being out also means the excavator motion cache cannot be refilled, so 6g's per-sample sums cannot be rerun. This step is therefore what the committed data alone can answer.
- Model, fixed now: per temperature, loss in "bar of full-stroke flow" L(x, p) = x p (1/eta - 1), fitted by ordinary least squares as L = k0 + kp p + kl p^2 + kc x p (k0: speed-dependent drag at zero stroke; kp: pressure-proportional friction; kl: leakage, flow proportional to p so power to p^2; kc: friction torque proportional to displacement and pressure). All 60 cells of `kauranne2022_fig13_total_efficiency.csv` per temperature (6 pressures x 10 digitised strokes). eta_model = x p / (x p + L).
- The bound, derived before any number: for loss linear in x, L = A(p) + B(p) x, the flat hold charges x L(x0)/x0 below x0 where the model charges A + B x; the shortfall is A (1 - x/x0). So per hour of load-sensing work the uncharged pump fuel is (b / 600) Q_max A(p + m) g, with g = E[(1 - x/x0)+] over the moving samples (0 <= g <= s, s the time share below x0, q < 58.4 L/min on the XE80E), and the break-even f_t* rises by that over (tau - 1) at every pump size (a lower bound on the rise of f_t,max). Q_max = 219 L/min (the XE80E pump scaled by displacement fraction, as 6g), p + m = 150 bar, b 0.2203, central tau 1.144. g itself needs the motion trace (blocked); it is reported as a curve.
- **Predictions:**
  - K1: the 4-term model fits each temperature's 60 cells with an RMS efficiency residual of at most 0.015. Prior 60%.
  - K2: fitted on the strokes of 15 cm3/r and up only, it predicts the 12 cm3/r curve within 0.02 of efficiency at every pressure from 50 to 250 bar, at 60 C. Prior 60%.
  - K3: at 60 C and 150 bar the stroke-independent loss A is between 30% and 70% of the full-stroke loss A + B. Prior 55%.
  - K4 (close to arithmetic once A is known): at the central geometry (tau 1.144) the flat hold's shortfall exceeds 1 L/h on f_t* once g >= 0.10. Prior 55%.
  - K5: at 60 C the model's efficiency at x = 0.10 and 150 bar is below 0.55 (the flat hold says 0.672). Prior 60%.

### Outcome of 6h (`experiments/pump_loss_model.py`, `experiments/pump_loss_model_results.txt`, under 1 s, stdlib; `measured.fit_pump_loss`, `measured.loss_model_eta`, `measured.flat_hold_shortfall`, 1 new test that recovers a known model exactly, 62 in all)
- Fit (60 C): L = 7.86 &minus; 0.0076 p + 5.26e-4 p^2 + 0.0350 x p bar (25 C: 10.55, &minus;0.0099, 3.50e-4, 0.0412). The small negative kp is unphysical alone and is absorbed by kl; dropping the 25 bar row barely moves A at 150 bar (18.55 to 18.56).
- **K1 refuted, narrowly:** RMS efficiency residual 0.0156 at 60 C (0.0104 at 25 C), max 0.043. The residual curves with stroke: about &minus;0.01 at 20 to 30 cm3/r and +0.021 at 45, so the model reads full stroke 0.863 against the measured 0.840. A loss linear in x is not the whole shape; the measured pump gains less from 0.8 to 1.0 of stroke than the model lets it.
- **K2 refuted:** fitted on 15 cm3/r and up, the 12 cm3/r prediction misses by up to 0.046 (250 bar) and 0.042 (25 bar); at the 100 to 200 bar loads this machine works at it is within 0.022 (&minus;0.009, +0.004, +0.022), and at the central 150 bar within 0.004. So the downward extrapolation is good to a few points near the working pressure and not at the extremes.
- **K3 refuted:** at 150 bar the stroke-independent loss A is **0.78** of the full-stroke loss (18.55 of 23.79 bar of full-stroke flow; 0.73 at 25 C), above the 0.30 to 0.70 predicted. Most of this pump's loss does not shrink with stroke.
- **K4 held (arithmetic):** the flat hold understates the central geometry's break-even f_t* by **10.36 g L/h** (b 0.2203, Q_max 219 L/min, tau 1.144), so by 1.04 L/h at g = 0.10; a rise of 1 L/h needs g = 0.097.
- **K5 held:** the model gives **0.440** at x = 0.10 and 150 bar (60 C), against the flat hold's 0.672 (0.285 at 0.05, 0.538 at 0.15, 0.605 at 0.20).
- Zero-stroke drag carried to the XE80E pump by displacement fraction: 6.8 kW at 150 bar, 1.49 L/h of fuel (60 C).
- Post hoc (labelled; added after the residual pattern showed): A fitted on the small strokes alone (12 to 25 cm3/r) is larger, 23.7 bar (4-term fit) or 20.1 bar (a line on the 150 bar row), against 18.55 from the full range; the rise per unit g becomes 11.2 to 13.3 L/h. The full-range A is the low end, so the bound above is conservative.
- Reading: holding efficiency flat below 12 cm3/r was not a small courtesy. Three quarters of this pump's loss at 150 bar does not scale with stroke, so at a tenth of stroke it runs at about 0.44, not 0.67, and each 0.01 of g (the mean shortfall of stroke below 0.267, over moving time) adds at least about 0.1 L/h to the central break-even. The direction is settled: 6g's 5.50 L/h central threshold, and its 17 to 33% of geometries where a fixed pump wins, are lower bounds. The size needs g from the motion trace (one line in `excavator_part_stroke_eta.py` once huggingface.co is reachable). The same constant drag also argues the other way at the stock pump size: a fixed pump that is oversized pays it at full stroke anyway, already inside its 0.84.
- Limits: one digitised pump, a loss model linear in stroke that misfits the top of the stroke range by 0.02; the bound charges only the stroke-independent loss and leaves load-sensing's standby flow and compensator uncharged; the motion-dependent g was not computed (huggingface.co, and every data host tried for a gear-pump curve, unreachable from this session).
- Next: (a) compute g per geometry and rerun 6g with `loss_model_eta` below x0 (needs the motion cache); (b) unchanged from 6g, a full-stroke efficiency for a fixed gear or bent-axis pump of the class at 150 bar; (c) a loss term in x^2 or a flow-loss term to fix the top-of-stroke misfit before the model is used above x0.

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 6g: a measured efficiency-against-displacement curve replaces the one borrowed pump efficiency, so load-sensing pays for running at part stroke (predictions written after the figure was seen, before it was digitised or any case computed)
- Why: 6d found the parameter half of the doubt (marginal slope b and pump efficiency eta) moves the excavator threshold as much as the cylinder geometry; 6f sourced b (0.2116 to 0.2203 L/kWh). eta is still the loader's record-wide 0.818, used for both circuits. That hides a known asymmetry, listed in the paper's threats (item 7) but never measured: a fixed pump runs at full stroke, while a load-sensing variable pump spends most of its time at part stroke, where axial piston pumps lose efficiency. On the excavator the load-sensing pump's median demand is about 90 L/min against its 219 L/min maximum, about 0.4 of full stroke.
- Source, opened today: Kauranne, *Effect of Operating Parameters on Efficiency of Swash-Plate Type Axial Piston Pump*, Energies 15(11):4030 (2022), doi:10.3390/en15114030, CC BY (Aalto repository PDF). A nine-piston variable pump, 45 cm3/r maximum, 280 bar nominal, measured to ISO 4409 with the swash plate **locked** at 45, 33, 23 and 12 cm3/r (so each curve is literally a fixed-displacement pump at that stroke), 25 to 250 bar, 500 to 2000 r/min, ISO VG 46 at 25 and 60 C inlet. Figure 13 plots total efficiency against derived capacity at 1500 r/min for six pressures, both temperatures. The paper has no data release ("Data Availability Statement: Not applicable"), so the curves are digitised from the figure.
- What was seen before writing these predictions: Figure 13 itself (by eye at 60 C and 150 bar: about 0.67 at 12 cm3/r, 0.76 at 20, 0.83 at 30, 0.84 at 45), and a rough mental break-even (a 150 bar load-sensing outlet at about 0.76 against a full-stroke fixed pump at 0.84 moves the break-even margin from about 20 to about 36 bar). No script has run on the curves or the motion.
- Method fixed now: (1) digitise both panels of Figure 13 by line colour (axes calibrated on the printed gridlines, 10 to 50 cm3/r and 30 to 100%), check against the by-eye endpoints; read error to be reported. (2) eta(x, p): x = displacement fraction V/45, linear in x between the digitised points, held at the 12 cm3/r value below x = 0.267 (this flatters load-sensing, whose real efficiency keeps falling towards zero stroke), linear in pressure between the 25 to 250 bar curves. (3) Carried to the XE80E's 104.3 cm3 pump by displacement fraction (an assumption: same efficiency shape at another size and at 2100 instead of 1500 r/min). (4) Load-sensing shaft energy per sample: q (p + m) / eta(min(q, 219)/219, p + m) over the moving samples, so its effective efficiency is the flow-weighted harmonic mean eta_LS. Open-centre: the fixed pump at full stroke, eta_OC = eta(1, p + m*), m* = 0.78 (r - 1) p as 5u. (5) Per hour of load-sensing work the fuel gap is (b / 600) Q_LS [(p + m*) / eta_OC - (p + m) / eta_LS] + f_t (tau - 1); f_t* zeroes it and f_t,max is its maximum over the pump grid (with eta_OC = eta_LS this is 6c's formula; checked). Central: 60 C (21 cSt, a working temperature), m 20 bar, p 130 bar, b 0.2203 (6f); 25 C, b 0.2116 and 0.2332, and the 6c pressure and margin sweep as sensitivities. (6) Compared against 6f's carried no-load rates at 2100 rpm (3.99 to 5.06 L/h).
- **Predictions:**
  - G1: the flow-weighted load-sensing efficiency at the central geometry (60 C, 150 bar outlet) lies in 0.72 to 0.80, against a full-stroke 0.82 to 0.86. Prior 70%.
  - G2: at the central geometry f_t,max is at least twice 6c's 2.462 L/h (rescaled to b 0.2203: 2.326). Prior 60%.
  - G3: the median f_t,max over the 384 geometries at central parameters is at least 2.0 L/h (6f's rescaled median: 0.945). Prior 60%.
  - G4: a smaller fixed pump beats 6f's lowest 2100 rpm no-load rate (3.99 L/h) in at least 10% of the 384 geometries at central parameters (6f: 0%). Prior 45%.
  - G5: at the stock 219 L/min pump open-centre still loses in every geometry (m* above the break-even margin with the efficiency asymmetry). Prior 90%.
  - G6: at 25 C the central-geometry f_t,max is within 25% of the 60 C value. Prior 50%.

### Outcome of 6g (`experiments/digitise_kauranne_eta.py` -> `experiments/data/kauranne2022_fig13_total_efficiency.csv`; `experiments/excavator_part_stroke_eta.py`, `experiments/excavator_part_stroke_eta_results.txt`, 52 s with numpy and h5py; `measured.eta_table`, `measured.part_stroke_eta`, `measured.breakeven_rest_rate_two_eta`, 1 new test)
- Before anything new ran, 6c's script was rerun to refill the motion cache; its output and its case table equal the committed files byte for byte.
- Digitising: axes calibrated on the plot frames (checked on the 60% and 80% grid lines to 1 px); 12 curves read by colour. Where a curve is drawn over by another (the 100 to 250 bar curves overlap) it is placed on the covering curve: 93% of the 25 C, 150 bar curve (under the 200 bar one), 47% of the 60 C, 100 bar and 26% of the 60 C, 150 bar curve. Read error about 0.5 efficiency points where visible, up to about 1 point where covered. By-eye endpoints confirmed (60 C, 150 bar: 0.672 at 12 cm3/r, 0.840 at 45).
- A first run's "equal efficiency" check failed (3.03 against 6c's 2.33 L/h): it set eta_LS to 0.818 but still took eta_OC from the curve (0.84). Fixed so both are 0.818; it then reproduces 6c to 1e-9 and asserts so. The failed check was itself informative: 2 points of fixed-pump efficiency moved the central threshold by 30%.
- **G1 held:** the flow-weighted load-sensing efficiency at the central geometry is **0.769** (60 C, 150 bar outlet), against **0.840** at full stroke; over the 384 geometries 0.681 to 0.791 (median 0.734).
- **G2 held:** the central f_t,max is **5.50 L/h** (at a 101 L/min fixed pump, tau 1.144), 2.36 times 6c's 2.33 at the same b.
- **G3 held:** the median over the 384 geometries is **3.32 L/h** (p10 2.07, p90 5.84, range 1.42 to 9.37), against 0.945 with one efficiency.
- **G4 held:** a smaller fixed pump beats 6f's lowest 2100 rpm no-load rate (3.99 L/h) in **32.8%** of geometries at central parameters, and its highest (5.06 L/h) in 17.2% (6f: 0% for both).
- **G5 held:** at the stock 219 L/min pump open-centre burns more pump fuel in every geometry (by 3.1 to 7.9 L/h of work, median 5.3).
- **G6 held:** at 25 C the central f_t,max is 5.94 L/h, 8% above 60 C; over the geometries 22.4 to 39.1% beat the 6f rates.
- Sweep (4 margins x 3 pressures x 3 b, eta now from the curve): at 3.99 L/h a fixed pump wins under every set in 6.2% of geometries and under at least one in 84.1%; at 5.06 L/h, 2.6% and 65.9%.
- How fragile (added after the first run, labelled so): the fixed pump's efficiency at which load-sensing wins back. At the central geometry a fixed pump at **0.962 of the curve's full-stroke value (0.808 instead of 0.840)** loses again at 3.99 L/h, and at 0.990 (0.831) at 5.06 L/h; over the geometries where a fixed pump wins, the median is 0.968 and the lowest 0.899. So a fixed pump 1 to 10 points less efficient than this piston pump at full stroke (a gear pump, or a smaller unit) hands every geometry back to load-sensing.
- Reading: the one-efficiency model hid the largest single effect found since 6c. A load-sensing pump on this excavator spends its time near 0.4 of stroke, where the measured pump is 7 to 10 points less efficient than at full stroke, and on a 130 bar load that costs more than the 20 bar margin does; with it the fuel ranking that 6f closed at central parameters reopens in a third of geometries. But the reversal rests on a fixed pump as efficient as the best point of a piston pump; at 3 to 4 points less, load-sensing wins again. The ranking now turns on two efficiencies of the actual pumps, not on the engine.
- Limits: one 45 cm3/r pump (Kauranne does not name its make), scaled to the XE80E's 104.3 cm3/r by displacement fraction, at 1500 r/min not 2100; the curve below 12 cm3/r (0.27 of stroke) is held flat, which flatters load-sensing; the fixed pump is the same piston unit locked at full stroke, not a gear pump; a real load-sensing pump adds standby flow and compensator losses that are not charged; one constant load pressure (130 bar) sets every efficiency read.
- Next: (a) a full-stroke efficiency for a fixed gear or bent-axis pump of the class at 150 bar (datasheet or test), the number the ranking now hinges on; (b) a part-stroke efficiency for a load-sensing pump at about 100 cm3/r, to check Kauranne's shape at the XE80E's size; (c) the paper's threats item 7 now has a number (done in this step, section 3.7h).

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 6f: the no-load fuel of a 3 to 4 L class diesel from the tractor-test standard, replacing 6e's floor (predictions written after the source was read, before any script ran)
- Why: 6e's floor (at least 2.53 L/h at 2100 rpm on the XE80E's Kubota V3307) rested on an unsourced assumption (no-load fuel per revolution does not fall with speed) and one 83 kW loader engine at low idle. Its Next (a) asked for a measured no-load or part-load fuel line of an industrial diesel. The Nebraska Tractor Test Laboratory measures exactly that: every OECD test's "Varying power and fuel consumption" table runs the engine at full throttle from rated power down to about 0% of it (the high-idle, no-load row). The test PDFs on digitalcommons.unl.edu sit behind a bot challenge this session will not work around (403 to WebFetch), but the general model fitted to those tests is published in an open extension factsheet that did download: Grisso, Perumpral, Vaughan, Roberson and Pitman, *Predicting Tractor Diesel Fuel Consumption*, Virginia Cooperative Extension publication 442-073 (2010), vtechworks.lib.vt.edu, opened and read today. It gives (eq. 2, the ASABE D497.6 general model, "based on the average results from more than 500 tractors") Q_F = (0.0434 X + 0.019) P_PTO gal/h at full throttle, X the fraction of rated PTO power (hp), and (eq. 3) Q_R = Q_F [1 &minus; (N &minus; 1)(0.45 X &minus; 0.877)] at reduced throttle, N the ratio of reduced to full-throttle engine speed; and it reprints the whole varying-power table of NTTL test 1725 (John Deere 7610, 86.47 kW rated PTO, rated 2100 rpm, high idle 2267 rpm), whose last row is 0.77 kW at 7.77 L/h. The underlying paper is Grisso, Kocher and Vaughan 2004 (Applied Engineering in Agriculture 20(5), 553-561, doi 10.13031/2013.17455; not opened, its abstract read on agris.fao.org).
- In SI: Q_F = (0.2203 X + 0.0965) P_rated L/h with P_rated in kW. So the general model says a diesel at full throttle and no load burns 0.0965 L/h per rated kW, and its marginal slope is 0.2203 L/kWh (5n's loader slope, used by 6b to 6e: 0.2332).
- What was seen before writing these predictions: the two equations, their coefficients and the 1725 table (a rough mental product, 0.0965 x 54.6 = about 5.3 L/h, was formed while reading). Nothing below was computed by a script. So H2 and H3 are close to arithmetic, and the informative ones are H1, H4 and H5.
- Method fixed now: (1) ordinary least squares of the six 1725 rows, fuel (L/h) on PTO power (kW), at full throttle. (2) The general model carried to the XE80E: P_rated = k x 54.6 kW, k = 1 (excavator pump on the flywheel, central) and 0.85 (if the tractor PTO rating should be read as 85% of engine power; a sweep, not sourced); full-throttle no-load speed HI = 2200, 2375 (2200 x 2267/2100, the 1725 tractor's governor overshoot) and 2400 rpm; working speed n = 2100 rpm (pump rating), also 1500, 1800 and 2200; no-load rate (0.0965 k 54.6) [1 + 0.877 (n/HI &minus; 1)], eq. 3 at X = 0. (3) 6c's per-case thresholds rescaled by 0.2203 / 0.2332 (b only; eta stays 0.818) at central parameters, and 6c's sweep maximum (108 sets, b 0.20 to 0.26) unrescaled for the at-least-one-set count. (4) A check against the loader: eq. 3 at the loader's low idle (800 rpm, 83 kW, HI swept 2200 to 2400) against 6e's measured 1.463 L/h.
- **Predictions:**
  - H1: the 1725 fit has slope 0.19 to 0.23 L/kWh and intercept 6.5 to 8.5 L/h (0.075 to 0.098 per rated kW). Prior 75%.
  - H2: at 2100 rpm the carried no-load rate lies in 3.5 to 5.5 L/h in all six (k, HI) scenarios, above 6e's 2.53 L/h floor in every one. Prior 80%.
  - H3: at central parameters (b 0.2203) a smaller fixed pump wins in 0% of the 384 geometry cases in every 2100 rpm scenario. Prior 90%.
  - H4: at the lowest 2100 rpm scenario, a fixed pump wins under at least one of the 108 parameter sets in under 5% of cases. Prior 70%.
  - H5: at 1500 rpm (eq. 3 extrapolated to N about 0.63 to 0.68), the at-least-one-set share stays under 15% in every scenario. Prior 55%.
  - H6: eq. 3 extrapolated to the loader's low idle over-predicts the measured 1.463 L/h by more than 1.5 times. Prior 70% (not blind: a rough product was formed while reading).

### Outcome of 6f (`experiments/asabe_no_load_fuel.py`, `experiments/asabe_no_load_fuel_results.txt`, under 1 s, stdlib; `measured.asabe_fuel_lph`, 1 new test that reproduces the factsheet's own worked example to its printed digits, 60 in all)
- **H1 held:** the six full-throttle rows of NTTL test 1725 fit fuel = **7.714 + 0.2116 P** L/h (P in PTO kW; max residual 0.25 L/h), an intercept of **0.0892 L/h per rated kW**. The general model (0.0965 per kW, slope 0.2203 L/kWh) reads 4 to 10% high on every row of this tractor (8.51 against 7.77 L/h at 0.77 kW), so it is not a low estimate. The tractor's own "specific" model from its 50% and 75% pull tests extrapolates to 8.91 L/h at no load, 15% above the measured row: the no-load intercept has to come from a no-load measurement, which is why the general model's (fitted to varying-power tests that include it) is used.
- The marginal slope, which 6d found is half the parameter doubt: the measured tractor gives 0.2116 L/kWh and the 500-tractor model 0.2203, both within 9% of the loader's 0.2332 that 6b to 6e borrowed. 6c's thresholds rescaled to 0.2203: median 0.945, max 2.838 L/h.
- **H2 held:** carried to the XE80E (rated 54.6 kW x k, k 1 or 0.85; full-throttle no-load speed 2200, 2375 or 2400 rpm), the no-load rate at 2100 rpm is **3.99 to 5.06 L/h**, 1.58 to 2.00 times 6e's 2.53 L/h floor; the floor was a floor. The 1725 tractor's measured intercept carried by rated power gives 4.87 L/h.
- **H3 held:** at central parameters a smaller fixed pump wins in **0% of the 384 geometries** in every scenario, at every working speed from 1500 to 2200 rpm (the lowest carried rate, 3.00 L/h at 1500 rpm, is above the rescaled maximum 2.84).
- **H4 refuted:** at the lowest 2100 rpm scenario (k 0.85, 3.99 L/h) a fixed pump wins under at least one of the 108 parameter sets in **9.4%** of cases (k 1: 1.6 to 3.6%).
- **H5 refuted:** at 1500 rpm the at-least-one-set share is **11.2% to 24.2%**.
- **H6 held (not blind):** eq. 3 at the loader's low idle predicts 3.33 to 3.54 L/h against the measured 1.463, **2.27 to 2.42 times** too high. The reduced-throttle factor was fitted on loaded drawbar tests at N about 0.76; taken to N 0.35 and X 0 it overstates idle fuel. So the 1500 rpm rows (N 0.63 to 0.68) are probably high too, and a fixed pump's chances there are probably understated; at 2100 rpm (N 0.88 to 0.95) the extrapolation is short.
- Reading: with a sourced no-load fuel rate, load-sensing keeps the excavator's fuel lead at central parameters in every geometry and every working speed tried. A smaller fixed pump survives only at parameter corners (low marginal slope, high pump efficiency, high margin): 1.6 to 9.4% of geometries at pump-rating speed, more if the engine slows while it waits. The open question is now narrow: an excavator that drops its engine speed while a slow fixed pump finishes the cycle (auto-idle does this) at the corner parameters.
- Limits: a tractor-population model, not an excavator engine (tractor no-load includes transmission and PTO drag the excavator lacks, and the excavator adds a pilot pump and a larger fan the tractor may not); the PTO-to-engine power ratio k is swept, not sourced; the XE80E's high-idle speed is not in its brochure (swept 2200 to 2400); the NTTL report PDFs themselves were not opened (bot challenge), only the table the factsheet reprints.
- Next: (a) an NTTL or OECD report for a tractor with a 3.3 L Kubota engine, to replace the population model with the engine class's own varying-power rows; (b) the XE80E's high-idle and auto-idle speeds (operator's manual); (c) a measured pump efficiency for the class, the other half of 6d's parameter doubt.

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 6e: what does an engine burn when the hydraulics do no work? A no-load fuel line measured on the loader from pump-side power, carried to the XE80E's engine (predictions written before the data were read)
- Why: since 6b the excavator's fuel ranking has turned on one number, the fuel the rest of the machine burns per hour of extra working time (f_t), and 6b to 6d borrowed the loader's 1.48 L/h (5th percentile of 1 s fuel rates) and 5.15 L/h (rest of machine, includes driving). 6b's Next (a), a sourced no-load fuel rate for the excavator's engine class, failed on every host tried: today the NCSU repository and core.ac.uk refused; the NCDOT/Frey reports that did open (FHWA/NC/2006-55, the EI16 slides) give cycle-average gal/h for three excavators but no idle row; CARB's hybrid-excavator report (Johnson et al. 2013) gives idle CO2 for 20 t machines in bar charts only; Kubota's V2403 sheets give full-load curves only. 5n's Willans line on the loader could not serve (intercept -0.94 L/h), because it regressed fuel on the ECU's own power estimate, which is itself built from fuelling.
- What is new: the loader record (Linjama et al., Zenodo 10.5281/zenodo.10639199) also carries the authors' shaft-power estimates of all four pumps (work, side, HST, HST boost; summed in `Data.AllPumps`), built from pressures, speeds and loss models, not from fuelling. Regressing the ECU fuel rate on that pump-side power and engine speed gives a Willans line whose intercept is the engine's fuel at zero pump load (friction, fan, alternator), which is what f_t should be on an excavator waiting for its hydraulics.
- Engine of the excavator (sourced, opened today): XCMG XE80E brochure (Europe, the high-config single-boom sheet): Kubota V3307CRTE5, 4 cylinders, 3.3 L, 94 x 120 mm, rated 54.6 kW at 2200 rpm, 265 N m at 1500 rpm; main pump 219 L/min at 2100 rpm. The loader's engine: 83 kW (`Data.Diesel.Params.EnginePower`); its displacement is not in the record.
- Method fixed before the run: 1 s means of ECU fuel rate (L/h), engine speed n (rpm) and pump-side shaft power P (kW, `AllPumps`; if that sum is absent, the four pumps summed). OLS fuel = a + c (n - 2100) + b P; a is the no-load fuel at 2100 rpm (the XE80E's pump-rating speed; if 2100 rpm lies outside the record's 5th to 95th percentile of speed, the nearest edge is used and said so). 95% intervals by resampling the 18 Y-cycles (cycle_starts on lift position, as 5w) 2,000 times, seed 0. Robustness: the same fit on seconds with P under 10 kW. Carried to the XE80E two ways: (i) scaled by rated power, a x 54.6 / 83 (central); (ii) unscaled a (the loader's own engine, an upper reading). The excavator thresholds of 6c scale linearly in b (f_t* = b (m - m*) Q_LS / (600 eta (tau - 1))), so 6c's per-case f_t,max at central parameters is multiplied by b / 0.2332 before the comparison; eta stays at 0.818.
- **Predictions:**
  - F1: the no-load intercept a at 2100 rpm lies in 1.5 to 4.0 L/h. Prior 55%.
  - F2: the marginal slope b on pump-side shaft power lies in 0.20 to 0.28 L/kWh (5n's slope on the ECU power: 0.2332). Prior 60%.
  - F3: no-load fuel rises with engine speed: c > 0 and its 95% cycle-resampled interval excludes 0. Prior 80%.
  - F4: carried to the XE80E (i), the no-load rate exceeds 6c's median threshold (rescaled by b / 0.2332), so at the median geometry load-sensing keeps the fuel lead. Prior 70%.
  - F5: at that carried rate (i), a smaller fixed pump wins (rescaled f_t,max above it) in under 20% of the 384 geometry cases. Prior 65%.
  - F6: the fit on seconds with P under 10 kW gives an intercept within 25% of the full fit's. Prior 55%.

### Outcome of 6e (`experiments/no_load_fuel.py`, `experiments/no_load_fuel_results.txt`, 2 s with numpy and scipy; `measured.no_load_line`, 1 new test, 59 in all)
- Checks: `AllPumps` equals the four pumps summed to 0.0000 kW; 440 s of 1 s means; engine speed p5 800, median 1566, p95 1950 rpm, so **2100 rpm lies outside the record's p5 to p95** (the method said to use the nearest edge; the script evaluated at 2100 anyway, an extrapolation, and the fit is void for the reason below regardless).
- **The registered fit failed.** fuel = 12.55 + 9.61 (n - 2100)/1000 + 0.0554 P L/h (R^2 0.55; cycle-resampled 95% interval of b 0.041 to 0.081). A diesel cannot turn fuel into shaft work at 0.055 L/kWh (about a quarter of the best engines' rate), so P misses most of the engine's load and the speed term absorbs it. **F1 refuted** (a 12.55 L/h, outside 1.5 to 4.0), **F2 refuted** (b 0.055, outside 0.20 to 0.28). F3 (c > 0, interval 8.9 to 12.2 per 1000 rpm) and F6 (low-load intercept 0.93 of the full one) held but are statements about a broken fit; **F4 and F5 rest on that fit and are void**, not held.
- Why it fails (post hoc, not registered): the pump-side estimates cover **35%** of the ECU's mean engine power (13.06 against 36.84 kW). The HST pump's shaft-power estimate averages -3.45 kW while its hydraulic power averages +6.36 kW, which a pump cannot do, and it correlates -0.59 with fuel. The record documentation says the HST torque estimate is "inaccurate because of simple torque loss model used". Taking |HST| instead (also post hoc) still gives b 0.148 and a negative intercept at 800 rpm. So this record cannot give the engine's no-load line from pump power: the drive side is not measured well enough.
- The one clean no-load reading (post hoc): at low idle with |pump-side power| under 3 kW (28 s, median 800 rpm) the ECU reads a median **1.463 L/h** (p25 1.45, p75 1.48), **30.5 mm^3 per revolution**; this is the 1.48 L/h "low" rate 5x took as the 5th percentile, now tied to a speed and a pump load.
- A floor from it (post hoc, an assumption, not a measurement): if zero-load fuel per revolution does not fall with speed (friction mean effective pressure and fan load rise with speed; not checked on this engine), no-load fuel at n is at least 1.463 x n / 800. Carried to the XE80E's V3307 by rated power (54.6 / 83 = 0.658): **at least 2.53 L/h at 2100 rpm** (1.80 at 1500, 2.16 at 1800, 2.65 at 2200); unscaled 3.84. Against 6c's thresholds (b 0.2332 unchanged): a smaller fixed pump beats that floor in **0.8% of the 384 geometries at central parameters** (3 cases; the central geometry's 2.462 sits just under 2.53) and under at least one of the 108 parameter sets in 38.8%; at the unscaled floor 0.0% and 11.2%.
- Reading: if the excavator's engine runs at its pump-rating speed while it waits, load-sensing keeps the fuel lead in essentially every geometry at central parameters. A fixed pump stays in play only if the engine drops well below working speed during the extra time (at 1500 rpm, 8.3% of cases) or at the parameter corners (low marginal efficiency or high margin). The open question has moved from "what does the engine burn at no load" to "what does the borrowed fuel slope and pump efficiency do", which 6d already found is half the doubt.
- Limits: one 83 kW loader engine at one low-idle speed, carried by rated power to a 54.6 kW engine of another make (displacements: 3.3 L for the V3307, not stated for the loader); the floor's monotone per-revolution assumption is unsourced; ECU fuel rates are not metered fuel.
- Next: (a) a measured no-load or part-load fuel map of a 3 to 4 L industrial diesel at 1500 to 2200 rpm, to replace the floor; (b) unchanged from 6d (d), the marginal slope and pump efficiency, now the larger half of the doubt; (c) the paper's section 3.7e to carry this floor.

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 6d: can a builder read the excavator threshold off two numbers of their own machine? A screening rule fitted to 6c's 384 cases (predictions registered before any fit was run)
- Why: 6c found the break-even rest-of-machine fuel rate f_t,max ranges 0.33 to 3.00 L/h over the 384 cylinder-geometry readings (median 1.00), falling as the stock pump's oversupply rises (Spearman -0.726 with r at 219 L/min). 6c's Next steps all need hosts this session cannot reach: at about 03:20 UTC the proxy refused huggingface.co (403 to CONNECT), and WebFetch could not resolve gaftp.epa.gov or typeset.io, where the PEMS excavator idle rates (Abolhasani et al. 2008) sit. So this step works from 6c's committed per-case table only (`experiments/excavator_breakeven_geometry_cases.csv`). The question: a builder who logs one working day of their own machine's flow can compute r(stock pump) and the peakiness p99/mean of demand. Do those two numbers give the threshold well enough to decide, without the full sweep?
- Rule fitted (fixed before any fit): ordinary least squares of ln f_t,max (central parameters) on ln(r219 - 1) and ln(p99/mean), plus a one-variable version on ln(r219 - 1) only. Out-of-sample error from grouped cross-validation: 16 folds, one per (boom, arm) lever-factor pair, so each held-out fold is a geometry the fit has not seen. Error measured as |predicted / actual - 1| per case. Decision check: at the loader's lowest measured rate, 1.48 L/h (borrowed, as 6b), the rule's call (fixed pump wins if the predicted f_t,max > 1.48) is compared with the exact call per case. Bounds: 6c's per-case sweep minimum and maximum (108 parameter sets) give, per f_t, the share of cases in which a fixed pump wins under every set, under some set, and at central parameters.
- **Predictions:**
  - E1: the one-variable rule on ln(r219 - 1) has in-sample R^2 above 0.5 on ln f_t,max. Prior 60%.
  - E2: the two-variable rule's grouped cross-validated median relative error is below 15%. Prior 50%.
  - E3: the two-variable rule gets the 1.48 L/h call right in at least 90% of the 384 cases. Prior 65%.
  - E4: at f_t = 1.48 L/h, under 5% of cases have a fixed pump winning under all 108 parameter sets (sweep minimum above 1.48). Prior 80%.
  - E5: at f_t = 1.48 L/h, more than 50% of cases have a fixed pump winning under at least one parameter set (sweep maximum above 1.48). Prior 55%.

### Outcome of 6d (`experiments/excavator_threshold_screening.py`, `experiments/excavator_threshold_screening_results.txt`, under 1 s, stdlib; `heavy_hydraulics.screening` (OLS, grouped CV), 1 new test, 58 in all; figure `experiments/threshold_decision_curve.svg`)
- **E1 held:** ln f_t,max on ln(r219 - 1) alone has R^2 0.595 (slope -1.08: the threshold falls roughly in proportion to the stock pump's surplus).
- **E2 refuted, narrowly:** the two-number rule (Rule B, f_t,max ~ 15.555 (r219 - 1)^-0.916 (p99/mean)^-2.374 L/h, in-sample R^2 0.749) has a grouped cross-validated median relative error of **17.3%** (p90 41.0%, max 120.3%). One variable alone: 25.3%.
- **E3 refuted, narrowly:** Rule B makes the right call at 1.48 L/h in **88.0%** of cases out of sample (338 of 384: 18 false "fixed wins", 28 missed fixed wins); one variable: 82.0%.
- **E4 held:** at 1.48 L/h no case (0.0%) has a fixed pump winning under all 108 parameter sets; the share is already 2.3% at 0.75 L/h and 0.3% at 1.0.
- **E5 held:** at 1.48 L/h, 72.1% of cases have a fixed pump winning under at least one parameter set (central parameters: 18.2%).
- Not registered, added after seeing B and labelled so: Rule C (Rule B plus a swing-share-0 indicator) reaches in-sample R^2 0.831 and CV median error 15.8% (max 66.5%), still above E2's 15%, and 87.5% right calls; swing's flow is a modelling choice the motion cannot fix, so a builder cannot read it off their machine anyway.
- The central geometry is where the rule is worst placed: Rule B gives 1.72 L/h against its exact 2.462, because that case sits at the 99th percentile (6c).
- Parameters against geometry: at the central geometry the 108 parameter sets move f_t,max from 0.89 to 5.24 L/h (x5.90); per case the spread is x5.13 to x9.24 (median x6.80). At central parameters the 384 geometries move it from 0.33 to 3.00 L/h (x9.21). The two sources of doubt are of the same size: neither alone settles the call. At 1.48 L/h the call is open in 72.1% of cases (a fixed pump wins under some parameter set but not under all), closed for load-sensing in 27.9%, and closed for a fixed pump in none.
- Reading: the threshold is mostly a function of how much oil the stock pump wastes (r219) and how peaky the demand is, but two numbers still leave a typical error of about one sixth, so calls near the line need the full computation (6c's script, 11 s per machine). The decision curve shows what a builder can say without either: below about 0.5 L/h a fixed pump wins on most readings (81.5% of geometries at central parameters, 21.4% under every set), above 2.5 L/h it loses on almost all (0.8% at central parameters), and between them the answer depends on the borrowed parameters as much as on the linkage. A first draft of this entry said the parameters move the answer more than the geometry; the spread check above, added before anything was committed, does not support that, and it was removed.
- Next: unchanged from 6c (a), (b), (c), plus (d): a measured marginal fuel slope (L/kWh) and pump efficiency for a 40 to 50 kW excavator engine and pump, which narrow the parameter half of the doubt as (c) narrows the geometry half.

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 6c: the break-even rest-of-machine fuel rate over 5z's 384 geometry cases, from the raw folder-75 motion (predictions written before any case was computed)
- Why: 6b's threshold (2.46 L/h at the central geometry) came from the committed central results only, because huggingface.co was refused that day; its Next (b) asks whether it survives 6a's 384 geometry cases (lever factors 1 to 2.5 per joint, bucket sign, swing share 0, 0.5, 1). At about 07:00 UTC on 2026-11-13 huggingface.co, zenodo.org and arxiv.org answered, so this step reads the motion again.
- Method: per case, the exact volume ratio r(q_p) from the moving-sample demand on a 0.5 L/min grid of pump sizes (no convexity bracket is needed with the data in hand), the time ratio by direct stretched-time sums (and checked against 6b's identity), the mean demand over load-sensing time per case, and f_t*(q_p) = b (m - m*) Q_LS / (600 eta (tau - 1)) with m* = 0.78 (r - 1) p (measured.breakeven_rest_rate). f_t,max is its maximum over q_p. Central parameters as 6b (m 20 bar, p 130 bar, b 0.2332 L/kWh, eta 0.818, b and eta borrowed from the loader); the 108-point sweep (m 14/20/24.9/25, p 100/130/174, b 0.20/0.2332/0.26, eta 0.75/0.818/0.95) is repeated for every case.
- **Predictions:**
  - D1: at the central geometry the exact f_t,max lies in 2.44 to 2.48 L/h (6b's bracket closed at 2.46). Prior 90%.
  - D2: the median over the 384 cases of f_t,max (central parameters) lies in 1.5 to 3.5 L/h. Prior 55%.
  - D3: fewer than 10% of the 384 cases have f_t,max (central parameters) above the loader's 5.15 L/h. Prior 70%.
  - D4: over the 384 cases, Spearman between demand peakiness (p99 over mean moving demand) and f_t,max (central parameters) exceeds +0.5. Prior 55%.
  - D5: in at least 80% of cases the maximising pump lies between 0.3 and 0.5 of that case's p99 demand. Prior 50%.
  - D6: over all 384 x 108 = 41,472 combinations, f_t,max stays below 10 L/h. Prior 60%.

### Outcome of 6c (`experiments/excavator_breakeven_geometry.py`, `experiments/excavator_breakeven_geometry_results.txt`, `experiments/excavator_breakeven_geometry_cases.csv`, 11 s with numpy and h5py; 1 new test, 57 in all)
- Before the script was written, 6a's script was rerun in the background to refill the data cache; its output equals the committed `measured_excavator_pump_size_results.txt` byte for byte, so the data read today is the data 6a read.
- Checks: central geometry mu 100.9 L/min and r(219) 2.175 as 6b; Q_LS 98.14 against 6b's 98.18 (6b worked from rounded inputs). 6b's identity tau(r) holds on every case at three pump sizes to 1e-9. The exact central f_t*(q_p) lies inside 6b's bracket at all ten tabulated pumps.
- **D1 held:** central exact f_t,max 2.462 L/h at 87.5 L/min (0.365 of p99).
- **D2 refuted:** the median over the 384 cases is **1.00 L/h** (p10 0.42, p90 1.71, range 0.33 to 3.00). The central geometry is at the 99th percentile (0.8% of cases above it).
- **D3 held:** no case above 5.15 L/h at central parameters; 18.2% above the loader's lowest measured rate, 1.48 L/h.
- **D4 refuted, with the opposite sign:** Spearman(p99/mean, f_t,max) **-0.589**; with r at 219 L/min -0.726. Within this machine, the more the stock pump oversupplies, the lower the threshold.
- **D5 refuted:** the maximising pump is in 0.3 to 0.5 of p99 in 62.5% of cases (range 0.207 to 0.470, median 0.316).
- **D6 held:** over 41,472 combinations the maximum is 6.67 L/h (median 0.96, p99 3.69); 0.1% exceed 5.15 L/h. The highest case is levers 1/1/1, bucket +1, swing 1 (central 3.00 L/h).
- By factor (median at central parameters): swing share 0 / 0.5 / 1 gives 0.49 / 1.28 / 1.15 L/h; bucket lever factor 1 to 2.5 gives 1.32 to 0.85; arm 1.21 to 0.86; boom 1.05 to 0.91; bucket sign +1 / -1 gives 1.18 / 0.90.
- Reading: 6b's 2.46 L/h was the most favourable geometry for open-centre, not a typical one; across the cylinder assumptions the fixed pump needs the rest of the machine to burn about 1 L/h or less over the extra time. 6b's sentence "the excavator's threshold is about 17 times higher [than the loader's] because its demand is peaky" holds as a comparison between machines (1.00 L/h median against 0.14) but not as a rule within one: across geometries, peakier demand gives a lower threshold, because the parity pump then slows more samples.
- 6b's Next (a) (a sourced no-load fuel rate for a 40 to 50 kW excavator engine) was tried again: one web search found only a dealer idle-cost table for a 336-class (36 t) machine and PEMS papers whose snippets give idle ratios, not litres per hour; none was opened, so no number is used.
- Next: (a) as 6b; (b) a measured engine fuel map for the class, which with this table settles the excavator's fuel ranking per geometry; (c) a cylinder-geometry check against an excavator with both motion and cylinder flow, to narrow the 384 cases.

## 2026-11-12 · Milestone 6b: the highest rest-of-machine fuel rate at which a smaller fixed pump still beats load-sensing on the measured excavator (predictions registered before any code was written; outcome below)
- Why: 6a found that on the measured excavator open-centre reaches load-sensing pump energy at 108 L/min for 11% more time, and its Next (a) asks whether that time costs more fuel than the pump energy saves. Folder 75 has no fuel or engine signal, so instead of pricing the time with one borrowed rate, this step computes the **break-even rate** f_t*: the fuel rate of the rest of the machine (engine friction, fan, accessories over the extra time) above which no fixed pump beats load-sensing. A builder compares one number from their own engine against it.
- Data: huggingface.co (folder 75) and zenodo.org were both refused by the proxy at 18:20 UTC (403 to CONNECT), so this step reads no raw data. It works from the committed `measured_excavator_pump_size_results.txt` and `measured_excavator_flow_results.txt` only, which is possible because of an identity found while planning (below).
- Identity (derived before the run): under 6a's slowing model a fixed pump runs flat out over the stretched time of every moving sample, so its pumped volume is q_p T_OC,moving, and r(q_p) = q_p T_OC,moving / V_d. Hence, with phi = still time over moving time and mu = mean moving demand, the time ratio is exactly tau(q_p) = (phi + r(q_p) mu / q_p) / (phi + r(219) mu / 219): the time cost is a function of the volume ratio alone. One row was checked by hand before this entry (87.6 L/min: 1.2320 against 6a's 1.232); no other row was computed.
- Between 6a's grid points r(q_p) is bracketed without data: E[max(q_p, q)] is convex in q_p with slope P(q < q_p), so 6a's "short %" column gives each tangent (lower bound) and the chords give the upper bound; below 87.6 L/min the chord runs to r = 1 at q_p = 0. f_t* falls as r rises at fixed q_p, so the two bounds bracket it.
- Break-even: per hour of load-sensing work, open-centre minus load-sensing fuel = b (m* - m) Q_LS / (600 eta) + f_t (tau - 1), with m* = 0.78 (r - 1) p (5u/5z), Q_LS the mean demand over load-sensing time (L/min), b the marginal fuel per shaft kWh and eta the pump efficiency. f_t*(q_p) = b (m - m*) Q_LS / (600 eta (tau - 1)) where m* < m; f_t,max is its maximum over q_p. Central: m 20 bar, p 130 bar, b 0.2332 L/kWh and eta 0.818 (both the loader's, 5n/5x, **borrowed, not measured on this machine**). Swept: m 14, 20, 24.9, 25; p 100, 130, 174; b 0.20, 0.2332, 0.26; eta 0.75, 0.818, 0.95. Reference rates, all borrowed from the loader (5x): low 1.48, rest of machine 5.15, still 5.99 L/h.
- A hand estimate from 6a's table before any code (87.6 L/min, central) gave about 2.4 L/h; disclosed because it informs C2.
- **Predictions:**
  - C1: the identity reproduces all 20 committed 6a time ratios (8 sweep rows, 12 equal-energy rows) within 0.002. Prior 80%.
  - C2: the bracketed f_t,max (central) lies between 1.5 and 4 L/h. Prior 65%.
  - C3: f_t,max stays below the loader's rest-of-machine 5.15 L/h in every swept case. Prior 70%.
  - C4: the maximising pump is below 98.5 L/min (0.45 of 219) at the central case. Prior 60%.
  - C5: at f_t 5.15 L/h the fuel-minimising fixed pump lies within 10% of q_eq (108.0 L/min) and costs under 0.7 L more than load-sensing per hour of load-sensing work. Prior 50%.

### Outcome of 6b (`experiments/excavator_fuel_breakeven.py`, `experiments/excavator_fuel_breakeven_results.txt`, 2 s, stdlib; `measured.time_ratio_from_r`, `measured.breakeven_rest_rate`, 2 new tests, 52 in all)
- Inputs read from the committed files: mean moving demand 100.9 L/min, still over moving time 0.0256 (97.5% moving), r(219) 2.175; mean demand over load-sensing time 98.18 L/min.
- **C1 holds.** The identity reproduces all 20 committed 6a time ratios within 0.0012 (rounding of the inputs). A test checks it exactly against the direct stretched-time sum, with a load-sensing pump that also slows.
- Bracket: chords and tangents agree at the 20 known points; r is uncertain only below 87.6 L/min (width 0.044 at 50, 0.062 at 70 L/min) and nearly fixed from there to 124 L/min, where 6a's equal-energy rows sit densely.
- **C2 holds.** Central f_t,max is **2.46 L/h**, at the 87.6 L/min pump (0.40 of the stock pump), where r is known, so the bracket is closed there. f_t* is 2.19 at 95, 1.37 at 102 and 0.28 L/h at 107 L/min, and 0.92 to 1.41 at 60 L/min.
- **C3 refuted, at one corner.** Over the 108 cases (margins 14 to 25 bar x p 100 to 174 bar x b 0.20 to 0.26 x eta 0.75 to 0.95) the upper bound of f_t,max runs 1.16 to 5.24 L/h. It passes the loader's 5.15 only at m 25 bar, p 100 bar, b 0.26, eta 0.75 (5.20 at 24.9 bar). At margins of 14 bar it is at most 1.99 L/h.
- **C4 holds** (87.5 to 87.6 L/min). **C5 holds:** at 5.15 L/h the fuel-minimising pump is 100.4 L/min (7% under q_eq) and open-centre still burns **0.515 L more per hour** of load-sensing work (0.587 at q_eq, 4.61 at the stock 219 L/min pump).
- The loader, for comparison (5x's committed numbers): its only pump below V_eq on the sweep, 25 cc, saves 8.7 mL of pump-energy fuel for 224 s more time, so f_t* is 0.14 L/h, under a tenth of the loader's lowest measured rate (1.48 L/h). The excavator's threshold is about 17 times higher because its demand is peaky.
- Reading: on the measured excavator the fuel ranking of a smaller fixed pump against load-sensing is no longer settled by the circuit. It turns on one engine number: the fuel the rest of the machine burns per hour of extra working time. Below about 2.5 L/h (1.2 to 5.2 across the sweep) a fixed pump near 0.4 of stock wins; above it, load-sensing wins. The loader's rest-of-machine rate (5.15 L/h) is above every central case, but it includes driving, which an excavator does not do while digging, so it is not a fair stand-in. On the loader the threshold is 0.14 L/h, under a tenth of its lowest measured fuel rate, so there the question was closed.
- Limits: b and eta are the loader's (swept); m* = 0.78 (r - 1) p is 5u's loader-fitted rule, used here at r 1.08 to 1.2; the excavator's flow and pressure come from different machines (5y, 5z); the extra time is charged at a constant rate; time is priced only in fuel, not in operator hours, which would favour load-sensing; the identity assumes 6a's slowing model (no engine speed-up and no change in how the operator drives).
- Next: (a) a sourced no-load fuel rate at working speed for a 40 to 50 kW excavator engine (an engine maker's fuel map, or a PEMS study's idle mode such as Abolhasani et al. 2008, J. Air Waste Manag. Assoc. 58, 1033, not yet read), to put one measured number against the 2.46 L/h threshold; (b) rerun 6b's sweep over 6a's 384 geometry cases when huggingface.co answers (the identity needs only r(q_p) per case); (c) paper section 3.7d (landed in this PR as a first version).

## 2026-11-12 · Milestone 6a outcome: on the excavator, open-centre reaches load-sensing pump energy at half the stock pump for 11% more time (the loader paid 39%)
- Why: 6a was registered earlier today (entry below) but could not read its data (huggingface.co refused by the proxy). At 17:07 UTC the host answered again, so this run executed the registered script unchanged: `python3 experiments/measured_excavator_pump_size.py > experiments/measured_excavator_pump_size_results.txt` (24 s including the range reads). No line of the script or of the registered setup was edited after the data was read.
- Checks the output carries: the numpy volume ratio equals the tested stdlib `pump_ratio` on file 1 to six decimals, and the inverse returns the pump it started from; r at the p99 pump (239.9 L/min) is 2.377, 5z's 2.38. Moving demand: mean 100.9, median 90.3, p99 239.9 L/min. Load-sensing, charged its own 219 L/min limit, slows 3.9% of moving samples.
- Pump sweep (central: kinematic signs, swing share 0.5, p 130 bar): 219 L/min gives r 2.175 and m* 118.8 bar at no time cost; 109.5 L/min (half) gives r 1.209, m* 21.1 bar and 1.108 times load-sensing's record time; 87.6 L/min (0.4) gives r 1.077, m* 7.8 bar and 1.232.
- **Equal-energy pump, central (20 bar margin, 130 bar): q_eq 108.0 L/min, 0.450 of the p99 demand and 0.493 of the stock pump, at 1.114 times load-sensing's record time.** Over margins 14 to 25 bar and p 100 to 174 bar it is 92.9 to 124.1 L/min at 1.069 to 1.191 times. At the loader's own measured margin (24.9 bar, 130 bar) it is 114.7 L/min at 1.092.
- Per file (pooled q_eq): time ratio 1.093 to 1.126, median 1.115; each file at its own q_eq 1.105 to 1.122.
- Geometry sweep, 384 cases x 3 margins x 3 pressures = 3,456: time ratio at q_eq 1.046 to 1.686 (median 1.197); q_eq / p99 0.220 to 0.647.
- **A1 held** (q_eq / p99 = 0.450, below 0.5). **A2 refuted** (1.114, not above 1.25). **A3 refuted** (1.114 against the loader's 1.39; 1.092 at the loader's margin). **A4 refuted** (min 1.046 over the sweep, below 1.10). **A5 held** (Spearman -0.842: files with more spare flow at the stock pump lose less time). **A6 held** (the first step, 219 to 197 L/min, buys 33.8 bar of break-even per % of time; the last, 109.5 to 108.0, buys 2.0; the last full sweep step, 131.4 to 109.5, buys 3.3).
- Reading: the excavator's demand is peakier than the loader's (median 90 against p99 240 L/min), so a fixed pump at half the stock size covers most samples and only the peaks slow. That is why the same trade that cost the loader 39% costs this excavator about 11% (5% to 69% across geometry). The equal-energy point is where m* equals the margin sold; the rule m* ~ 0.78 (r - 1) p is 5u's loader-fitted linearisation, and near r 1.2 it is used further from where it was checked than at r 2.4.
- What this does not show: fuel. 5x priced the loader's trade with the record's ECU fuel signal and found no fixed pump wins; folder 75 has no fuel or engine signal, so whether 11% more time costs more fuel than the pump energy saved is still H4's open question. The pressure (5y) and the flow (5z) still come from different machines, the levers are constant and the 10 Hz sampling misses short peaks (which would raise the time cost of a small pump).
- Next: (a) the time-to-fuel price of 5x on this demand, with the loader's fuel-rate readings as swept stand-ins (labelled as borrowed, not measured), to see whether the 11% shrinks or flips the fuel ranking; (b) paper section 3.7c from this entry (landed in this PR as a first version).

## 2026-11-12 · Milestone 6a: a smaller fixed pump on the measured excavator, priced in cycle time (predictions registered before any code was written)
- Why: 5z found that on measured excavator motion (folder `75` of `fuxi-robot/excavator-motion`, CC BY-NC-SA 4.0) a fixed pump sized at the p99 of demand delivers about 2.4 times the oil the work uses, so 5u's rule puts the break-even load-sensing margin at 70 to 140 bar. A builder choosing open-centre would fit a smaller fixed pump and accept slower peaks; 5w priced that trade on the loader (equal energy at 28.5 cc, 39% more time). 5z's Next: run the same trade on the excavator demand.
- An identity used throughout (derived before the run, from 5w's slowing rule): where demand q exceeds the pump flow q_p the sample stretches to dt q / q_p, so the pump volume is the sum of max(q_p, q) dt and r(q_p) = E[max(q_p, q)] / E[q] over moving samples. r rises monotonically from 1 (q_p to 0) and is unchanged if q and q_p are scaled together, so the time cost at a given r does not depend on a common lever scale; the pump size in L/min does.
- Setup fixed before the run: demand as 5z (qvel joint speeds, constant levers stroke / span, kinematic signs boom -1, arm +1; bucket +1 central, -1 swept; swing share s 0.5 central, 0 and 1 swept; moving = any joint above 0.05 rad/s). The break-even rule as 5z: m*(q_p) ~ 0.78 (r(q_p) - 1) p, central p = 0.41 x 316 = 130 bar, swept 100 to 174 bar (174 is 5y's absolute median). Equal-energy pump q_eq: the q_p at which m* equals a margin sold, central 20 bar, swept 14 and 25 (and 24.9, the loader's measured margin, for comparison with 5w), found by bisection on the identity. Time: open-centre record time sum max(1, q / q_p) dt over load-sensing's, which is charged its own pump limit (the published 219 L/min, sum max(1, q / 219) dt). Pumps also swept as fractions of 219 L/min: 1.0, 0.9, 0.8, 0.7, 0.6, 0.5, 0.45, 0.4. Geometry sweep as 5z's 384 cases (per-joint lever factors 1 to 2.5, bucket sign, swing share).
- **Predictions:**
  - A1: at the central case the equal-energy pump q_eq is below half the p99 of moving demand. Prior 60%.
  - A2: at q_eq (central) open-centre needs more than 1.25 times load-sensing's record time. Prior 50%.
  - A3: that time ratio exceeds the loader's 1.39 at its V_eq (5w). Prior 30%.
  - A4: over the 384 geometry cases x margins 14 to 25 bar x p 100 to 174 bar, the time ratio at q_eq stays above 1.10 in every case. Prior 50%.
  - A5: across the 10 files (central), Spearman(r at the 219 L/min pump, time ratio at q_eq) is below -0.5 (files with more spare flow lose less time). Prior 50%.
  - A6: as on the loader, the trade worsens with each step: the first step (219 to 0.9 x 219) buys more break-even bar per % of time than the last swept step before q_eq. Prior 80%.
