## 2026-11-03 · Milestone 5l: a published electric-excavator power profile (CWA pre-normative, Zenodo 18862594) as a shape check on the duty cycle (no prediction registered before the first run, stated honestly)
- Why: H4 still lacks an excavator pressure trace. A search of Zenodo found two excavator records: a Caterpillar 305.5E2 gauge and stopwatch test (Zenodo 17760471, CC BY, cylinder stroke times only, no pressures or trace, so unusable for H4) and the "Off-road electric power profile" (Arrinda, Popp, Chevillard, Michalow-Mauke, Zenodo 18862594, CC BY 4.0, 2026-03-04): raw electric power at 0.1 s for an electric excavator, part of a CWA pre-normative profile, three csv files (digging 80 s, grading 25 s, full 1,779 s). `experiments/cwa_profile.py` fetches them and prints shape statistics.
- Outcome (`experiments/cwa_profile_results.txt`): full profile mean 46.0 kW, peak 133.1 kW, **peak/mean 2.89**, p95/mean 2.20, best 3 s window 1.85 x mean, 22.7 kWh over 1,779 s (2.17 h per 100 kWh). Digging alone peak/mean 2.67, grading 2.36. Negative power (regeneration at the electrical terminals) is **0.001% of positive energy**.
- Reading: (1) a power-limited recovery or battery stage faces a peaky load: a 3 s window is up to 1.85 x the mean, in line with the 5e assumption that peaky profiles need more rating than a flat one, but this is total machine electric power, not boom-lowering power. (2) The near-zero negative power says the standard profile contains no modelled regeneration, so it can neither support nor refute 5a's recovery share (3.3% central); it is a drive-cycle input, not a measurement of recovery.
- Limits: machine size and class are not stated in the record (133 kW peak means far above the 1.5 to 3 t class); electrical input power, not hydraulic or cylinder pressure; I did not read the CWA document, so how the profile was made (measured or synthesised) is unknown to me; the script ran before I wrote a prediction, so none is registered. Nothing in the model changed.
- Next: read the CWA document for the profile's origin and machine; still need an excavator cylinder pressure trace for H4.

## 2026-11-03 · Milestone 5k: a measured excavator boom-lowering time brackets 5d's 2.5 s window (a sourcing check; no prediction registered)
- Why: the H4 pressure trace is still missing, so this run searched Zenodo's API (reachable from this egress) for measured excavator hydraulic data. Nothing with a pressure trace turned up. One open record gives measured stopwatch times for a real machine: Halim et al., *Int. J. Current Science Research and Review* 8(11):5863-5870 (2025), doi:10.47191/ijcsrr/V8-i11-43, Zenodo 10.5281/zenodo.17760471, CC BY 4.0, Caterpillar 305.5E2. No-load full-stroke test at engine dial 10: **boom cylinder retract (lowering) 2.10 s, manual specification 1.7 to 2.5 s**; extend 2.70 s (2.3 to 3.1 s).
- Result (`experiments/regen_cat_window.py`, `regen_cat_window_results.txt`, 5,000 force-model cycles): 5d's 2.5 s window is the top of the specified range. Median mean recovery power at 1.7 / 2.1 / 2.5 s is 1,210 / 979 / 823 W (central arm) and 4,296 / 3,478 / 2,921 W (upper corner); the 2.5 s rows reproduce 5d's 823 and 2,921 W. With 5g's 1.24 x mean rating for 95% capture: 1.5 / 1.2 / 1.0 kW (central), 5.3 / 4.3 / 3.6 kW (upper).
- Reading: a faster real lowering raises the rating needed. 5e's guide of 1 to 1.3 kW (central) and 3.4 to 4.5 kW (upper) was built on 2.5 s, so it is a floor against this machine's 2.1 s: the upper corner would need about 4.3 kW, still inside the 1 to 5 kW range priced in 5f. Conclusions of 5f (no fuel payback) do not change.
- Limits: unloaded stroke at maximum engine speed, not a loaded, operator-metered lowering (a loaded boom is usually slower and throttled), so this bounds the window from the fast side only; a 5.5 t machine against arms of 300 to 600 kg assumed; the paper is a maintenance test report in a low-tier journal, its Table 1 carries leftover text from another template and its jobsheet names a 320D2, so it is used only for the stopwatch times; no pressures or flows in it. Not an H4 test.
- Next: still an excavator pressure trace (H4); paper section 3.4a can cite the 2.1 s value.

## 2026-11-02 · Milestone 5i: where the PATU 655 cylinder bore is documented (a sourcing step, no experiment)
- Why: 5h swept the bore (80, 100, 125 mm) because the dataset files carry no geometry. Looked for the real figure.

It says "Cylinder dimensions and hydraulic parameters used for derived force computation match those reported in [1]", and [1] is Khadim et al., "State estimation in a hydraulically actuated log crane using unscented Kalman Filter", *IEEE Access* 10:62863-62878 (2022), doi:10.1109/ACCESS.2022.3179591 (gold open access; also in the LUT repository, lutpub.lut.fi/handle/10024/164458).
- Not done: this session could not read [1]. ieeexplore.ieee.org returned HTTP 418 through the proxy and the LUT handle page showed no PDF link. So the bore stays a swept guess (5h's 100/56 mm parts-listing reading is still unconfirmed), and no number changed.
- Next: read Table of cylinder parameters in Khadim et al. (2022) from a host that serves the PDF, replace the 80/100/125 mm sweep with the sourced bore and rod, and rerun `experiments/regen_watts.py`. An excavator pressure trace (H4) is still missing.

- Added the measured lowering shape (772 PATU 655 strokes: peak/mean 1.52, 95% rating 1.24, p90 1.61) and the bore-swept watts (0.78 / 1.21 / 1.89 kW median at 80 / 100 / 125 mm) to the draft, with limits (lab crane, unconfirmed bore, hydraulic power not recovered power). 5 new rows in `paper/check_numbers.py`, 72 claims, 0 missing.

## 2026-11-02 · Milestone 5h: absolute lowering power of the PATU 655 lift cylinder, with the cylinder bore swept (no prediction registered before the first run, stated honestly)
- Why: 5g gave only area-free shapes and ended "bores for watts". The dataset has no geometry. A spare-parts listing for the Patu 655 A names a "main lift cylinder 100/56 mm" seal kit (lectura-specs.com; read as bore/rod, **unconfirmed**), so `experiments/regen_watts.py` sweeps bore 80, 100 and 125 mm (rod 0.56 x bore) and computes the hydraulic power of each of 5g's 772 lowering strokes as `(PA1*A - PB1*(A - a)) * |dS1/dt|`. This also includes the rod-side pressure that 5g neglected.
- Outcome (`experiments/regen_watts_results.txt`): median stroke lasts 2.9 s (p10 0.9 s, p90 8.4 s), which matches 5d's 2.5 s lowering window. Median mean power per stroke is 0.78 / 1.21 / 1.89 kW at 80 / 100 / 125 mm bore (p10 to p90 at 100 mm: 0.64 to 2.2 kW); median peak 1.96 kW at 100 mm; median stroke energy 2.9 kJ. By payload at 100 mm the median mean power is 0.98 (95 kg), 1.15 (180 kg) and 1.75 kW (278 kg).
- Reading: against 5d's modelled excavator arm (median mean recovery power 0.82 kW central, 2.9 kW upper corner) the measured forestry-crane lowering is in the same range at the unconfirmed 100 mm bore (1.2 kW), so 5e's rating guide of 1 to 4.5 kW is not contradicted. With 5g's median 95% rating of 1.24 x the mean, a device taking 95% of a typical stroke would be rated about 1.5 kW at 100 mm (1.0 to 2.3 kW across the bore sweep). These are hydraulic powers at the cylinder, before any recovery efficiency, and a different machine from an excavator.
- Limits: bore is a swept guess anchored to one parts listing; the sign of net force is clipped at zero (strokes that push against the load are zeroed); lab scripted motions; hydraulic power is what the lowering releases, not what a device would recover; no friction separation.
- Next: confirm the bore from the PATU 655 manufacturer drawing or the dataset paper; an excavator trace remains missing.

## 2026-11-02 · Milestone 5g: do measured lowering strokes match 5e's assumed power shapes? (prediction registered before the first run)
- Why: 5e swept four assumed lowering-power shapes and said "a measured boom-lowering trace" was missing. The PATU 655 forestry-crane dataset (Saeed, Zhidchenko, Handroos; Mendeley Data `10.17632/rn5j42rpyr.1`, CC BY 4.0, published 2026-02-17) has 108 lab recordings of lift-cylinder piston-side pressure `PA1` and stroke `S1` at 95 to 278 kg payloads, so the power proxy `PA1 x |dS1/dt|` over each retraction (lowering) stroke gives a real profile. Cylinder areas are not in the files, so absolute watts are not available; the rating as a multiple of the stroke's own mean power does not need them (it is area-free), which is the quantity 5e tabulated. `experiments/regen_measured.py` finds strokes (smoothed 0.5 s, speed above 5 mm/s for at least 0.3 s, at least 20 mm travel) and reports peak/mean and the rating for 95% and 99% capture.
- **Prediction (before running):** the median over strokes of the 95%-capture rating lies inside 5e's assumed range (0.95 to 1.55 times the mean power), and the median peak/mean lies between 1.25 and 2.0. Prior 60%. Refuted if the median 95% rating is above 1.55 or the median peak/mean is above 2.0.
- Limits known in advance: lab crane with proportional valves and scripted step/ramp excitation, not an operator in a digging cycle; rod-side pressure neglected; the crane is a forestry crane (a different machine), so this tests the shape assumption only, not magnitude; whether S1 retraction is gravity-assisted at every posture is assumed from the stroke direction.

### Outcome of 5g (`experiments/regen_measured_results.txt`, 772 lowering strokes from 4 payloads: 95, 148, 180, 278 kg)
- Median peak/mean power is 1.52 (p10 1.31, p90 2.03); median rating for 95% capture is 1.24 times the stroke's mean power (p10 1.09, p90 1.61); for 99%, 1.38 (p90 1.88). Payload barely moves it (median 95% rating 1.15, 1.23, 1.24, 1.26 from 148 to 95 kg; the 148 kg folder has only 39 strokes).
- **The prediction held:** the median 95% rating (1.24) sits inside 5e's assumed 0.95 to 1.55 range, and the median peak/mean (1.52) inside 1.25 to 2.0. Measured strokes sit between 5e's trapezoid and half-sine shapes (1.17 and 1.35 for 95%). The tail is slightly beyond the assumed range: p90 of the 95% rating is 1.61 and of peak/mean 2.03, so about one stroke in ten needs a rating a little above 5e's worst assumed shape.
- Reading: 5e's rule of thumb "a rating 1.2 to 1.6 times the mean takes 95%" is supported on a real hydraulic boom's lowering power shape. It remains a shape check only: no absolute watts (areas not in the files), a different machine, scripted excitation.
- Not done: absolute power needs the PATU 655 cylinder bores (look in the dataset's paper); an excavator trace is still missing (the IEEE DataPort autonomous-excavation set is subscriber-only).
- Next: bores for watts; the paper's section 3.4a gains this outcome.

- Added the harvest-window cap (5d), the peaky-profile rating (5e), the electric-rung battery check (5c) and the fuel payback of a recovery unit (5f) to the draft as section 3.4a, with their limits. 9 new rows in `paper/check_numbers.py`, 67 claims, 0 missing.

## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 5f: does a 1 to 5 kW recovery e-machine pay back in fuel? (priced from retail listings, a floor; no prediction registered)
- Why: 5d and 5e bounded the rating at about 1 to 4.5 kW and left "price the device" open. `experiments/regen_price.py` combines retail prices for 48 V permanent-magnet generators (1.5 kW $819, 2 kW $289 EUR to $874, 5 kW EUR 1,599 with controller; listings seen 2026-11-01, wind/off-grid products) with 5a's recovery shares (3.3% central, 10% upper corner) applied to 5c's 2.3 kW mean shaft power, BSFC 250 g/kWh (assumed), $6.28/gal.
- Outcome (`experiments/regen_price_results.txt`): central saving 76 W, 0.023 L/h, $0.04 per hour; payback 8,200 to 45,600 h. Upper corner 230 W, $0.12 per hour; payback 2,700 to 15,100 h. Only the cheapest 2 kW unit at the upper corner is under 3,000 h; every central-duty case exceeds 8,000 h.
- Reading: on fuel alone the recovery device does not pay at the central duty; only a heavy, high-recovery corner with the cheapest hardware approaches a machine's service hours. Value would have to come from electric machines (battery size, 5c) or emissions, not diesel.
- Limits: retail wind-generator prices are not construction-grade drive units (a floor, so true payback is longer); no inverter/DC link or mounting; applying 10% to 2.3 kW understates the corner's own draw (so it flatters nothing but is not matched); BSFC assumed; not a quote or a measured machine.
- Next: a vendor quote for an automotive-grade 48 V motor-generator; the electric case where saved energy is battery kWh.

## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 5e: how much rated power does a peaky lowering profile need? (5d's "perhaps 2x more", measured; no prediction registered before the first run, stated honestly)
- Why: 5d's reading said a real, peaky lowering "needs perhaps 2x more" rated power than its flat-profile cap and marked it not measured. `experiments/regen_peaky.py` takes unit-energy profiles over the lowering (flat, trapezoid with 20% ramps, half-sine, triangle; shapes swept, no measured boom trace) and finds the rating that captures 95% and 99% of the energy, as a multiple of the mean power E/T. Stdlib, deterministic; checked by `tests/test_circuits.py::test_regen_peaky_rating_bounds` against the analytic triangle result 2 - sqrt(0.2).
- Outcome (`experiments/regen_peaky_results.txt`): peak/mean is 1.0, 1.25, 1.57, 2.0. The rating for 95% capture is 0.95, 1.17, 1.35, 1.55 times the mean power; for 99% it is 0.99, 1.23, 1.50, 1.80. A rating equal to the mean captures 100%, 84%, 79%, 75%. Applied to 5d's medians over 2.5 s: central (823 W) needs 0.78 / 0.96 / 1.11 / 1.28 kW for 95%; upper corner (2,921 W) needs 2.77 / 3.41 / 3.94 / 4.54 kW.
- **Reading: 5d's "perhaps 2x" is too pessimistic for 95% capture.** 2x is the peak of the worst shape (triangle) and is needed only to take 100%; a device rated 1.2 to 1.6x the mean takes 95% on every shape tried. So the rating guide becomes about 1 to 1.3 kW for a 300 kg arm and 3.4 to 4.5 kW for the heaviest corner. The 1 to 10% lever of 5a is still not limited by device size.
- Limits: shapes are assumed, not measured; a real lowering is operator-controlled and may have several sub-peaks or a stall, and the profile also depends on flow-control valve settings. Efficiency of the device at part load is not modelled. Not a measurement of any machine.
- Not done: the price of a 1 to 5 kW e-machine (needs catalogue prices); separating gravity from friction in the lift cap.
- Next: a measured boom-lowering trace (a test report or telematics) in place of assumed shapes; price the device against the 1 to 4.5 kW range now bounded.

## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 5d: does a harvest-window power cap change 5a's recovery share? (prediction registered before running)
- Why: 5a and the electric-rung entry (5c) both end "3 s harvest window". Boom lowering lasts seconds, so a recovery device (accumulator or e-machine) with rated power P_max can only take P_max x t_w of the lowering energy. `experiments/regen_window.py` reuses 5a's 5,000 force-model cycles and caps the recovered energy at min(potential x eta_rt, lift-phase shaft energy, P_max x t_w), for window t_w in 1.5, 2.5 (the model's lowering phase) and 3.0 s and P_max in 0.5, 1, 2, 5 and 10 kW (swept, not sourced). Mean-power cap on a flat lowering profile, so a peaky real profile is worse.
- **Prediction (before running):** at the central point (300 kg, 0.8 m, eta_rt 0.7) the mean recovery power is about 0.7 to 0.9 kW, so a 5 kW cap changes the 3.3% share by under 0.1 points, and only the 0.5 and 1 kW caps bite (central share under 2.5% at 0.5 kW). At the upper corner (600 kg, 1.2 m, 0.92) a 5 kW cap at 2.5 s cuts the 10.0% share by more than 1 point. Prior 65%. Refuted if the central 5 kW change is 0.1 points or more, or the upper-corner 5 kW cut is under 1 point.
- Limits known in advance: m_arm, dh, eta_rt, P_max swept; flat-profile mean-power cap; no accumulator sizing; no idle time.

### Outcome of 5d (`experiments/regen_window_results.txt`, 3 s, same 5,000 cycles)
- Median mean recovery power over 2.5 s is 823 W at the central point and 2,921 W at the upper corner. Central: no change from 3.33% / 3.21% (open-centre / load-sensing) at any cap of 1 kW or more with t_w 2.5 or 3 s; 0.5 kW gives 2.02% / 1.96% (under 2.5%). At t_w 1.5 s a 1 kW cap bites (2.43%) and 2 kW does not. Upper corner: 5 kW leaves 10.01% / 10.26% at 2.5 s and 3 s (and 9.99% at 1.5 s); 2 kW gives 7.9% at 2.5 s; 1 kW gives 4.0%.
- **Central half held; the upper-corner half is refuted:** I predicted a 5 kW cap would cut the corner by over 1 point at 2.5 s; it cuts it by 0.00 points, because the corner's mean power is 2.9 kW, well under 5 kW. The cap only bites below about 3 kW there.
- Reading: a recovery device needs rated power of roughly 1 kW for a 300 kg arm and 3 kW for the heaviest corner of this sweep to take the whole drop within a 2.5 s lowering; below that the share falls in proportion to power. A flat profile is the best case, so a real peaky lowering needs perhaps 2x more (not measured here). The 1 to 10% lever of 5a does not shrink for devices of ordinary size.
- Limits: swept masses, heights, efficiencies and ratings; flat profile; no accumulator sizing or price.
- Next: separate gravity from friction in the lift cap; price an e-machine of 1 to 5 kW (milestone 4 style).

## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 5c: the electric rung, what battery does the modelled duty cycle imply? (no prediction registered before the first run; stated here honestly)
- Why: milestone 5 names "the electric rung". The cheapest honest check is against a vendor claim: Volvo ECR25 Electric lists 20 kWh for up to 4 h and 40 kWh for up to 8 h (volvoce.com product page; indicative, depends on application), a mean battery draw of 5 kW. `experiments/electric_rung.py` takes milestone 4's mean pump-shaft power (load factor 0.346, force-model cycles, sourced margins) and divides by a swept motor+inverter+battery chain efficiency (0.80 to 0.92, assumed range).
- Outcome (`experiments/electric_rung_results.txt`, 1.5 s): mean shaft power is 2.3 kW (load-sensing, any p_n) and 2.3 / 2.7 / 3.4 kW (open-centre at p_n 8 / 20 / 40 bar). Battery draw 2.5 to 4.3 kW, so 20 kWh lasts 4.7 to 8.0 h, against the vendor's 4 h. Matching 5 kW would need a chain efficiency of 0.46 to 0.68, which is not physical.
- Reading: the model underestimates the vendor's mean draw by about 1.2 to 2x. Only at open-centre with p_n 40 bar and a poor chain (0.80) does it come within 20% of the claim. The gap is what the model leaves out (travel, auxiliaries and cooling, heavier duty than the 0.346 load factor, a bigger pump than 45 L/min), or conservatism in an indicative vendor runtime. It cannot be split with the data in hand. Electrifying removes the diesel's part-load inefficiency but, in this model, does not change the ranking of circuits: the OC/LS ratio is untouched because the chain efficiency is common to both.
- Limits: the vendor figure is marketing, one machine, not a measured trace; the chain efficiency is a swept assumption; the ECR25 pump size is unverified against the 45 L/min of the model. Not a measurement of any machine.
- Next: a measured battery-electric duty log (a field test report or telematics data) to split the 1.2 to 2x gap; regen on the electric rung (5a) with a 3 s harvest window.

## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 3p: the surplus fraction f of 3n is not free for a fixed pump (an identity, checked numerically)
- Why: 3n and 3o end "source f". Before hunting a bypass-flow curve, I asked whether f is a property of the valve at all. A fixed pump delivers Q at the pressure p it sees, and the surplus Q minus the actuator flows can only leave through the bypass at that same p, which must exceed the heaviest load (otherwise that actuator stalls). So pump power is Q p, the surplus is dropped at p, and the 3m charge (f = 1) is exact for a fixed pump, not the pessimistic end.
- Check (`experiments/fixed_pump_identity.py`, seeded, 2,000 random parallel phases with 1 to 3 actuators at 30 to 220 bar, solved by flow continuity with orifice spools and bypass): with the bypass sized for the surplus, the solved pressure equals p_max + drop to 6e-14 bar and the solved shaft energy equals `cycle_energy` open-centre to 5e-16 relative error. Widening the bypass by 1% to 2x lowers the pressure in all 8,000 cases, and in none of them do the actuators still get all their demanded flow. Pinned by `tests/test_circuits.py::test_fixed_pump_surplus_is_dropped_at_pump_pressure`. 15 tests pass.
- Consequence: the f < 1 rows of 3n (break-even 31.7 to 18.8 bar at 1.2 x) are not a fixed-pump open-centre circuit with a better valve. They describe a pump that delivers less than Q when demand is low (unloading, pressure-compensated or variable), which is the load-sensing family. For the fixed-pump circuit this programme calls open-centre, 3i, 3m (36.1 bar at 1.0 x, 44.5 bar at 1.2 x) stand with f = 1, and no bypass-flow curve can change them. The margin that the 14 to 30 bar range is read against is therefore decided by pump size and rated spool drop, both of which are sourced or swept.
- Limits: steady state, orifice laws, parallel circuit, no relief or pump leakage (a real pump's volumetric loss lowers delivered Q slightly at high p, which would make the charge a little lower); series-parallel or tandem centres and operator feathering are not modelled.
- Next: the paper's threats-to-validity wording for f; milestone 5 (the electric rung).

## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 3o: the "compensator" next step is already inside dp_ls (a model-scope check, no new sweep)
- Why: 3c to 3n each end with "add the compensator". Reading `cycle_energy`: load-sensing charges `q_dem * (p_max + dp_ls)`, so a pre-compensated section's throttling of a lighter actuator down from pump pressure to its own is already in the shaft energy, and the pump margin `dp_ls` is the whole drop from pump to the highest load (spool plus compensator in series, as in a PVG-type section). A separate compensator drop would only be a second `dp_ls` term, identical in effect to raising `dp_ls` by that amount, so no new sweep can change a break-even except by moving the margin the reader reads it against.
- Result: `tests/test_circuits.py::test_ls_compensator_loss_is_inside_the_margin` pins both facts (two actuators at 50 and 150 bar: exact shaft energy; +4 bar compensator equals +4 bar `dp_ls`). 14 tests pass. Consequence for the reading of 3f to 3n: the break-even margins (36.1 bar and so on) are on the **total** pump-to-load-pressure margin, so a reader comparing them with a datasheet margin must use the datasheet's pump margin and not subtract the compensator.
- Limits: this removes the compensator as an energy term only. What is still unmodelled is compensator dynamics, flow-sharing behaviour of a compensator under pump saturation beyond `flow_share_ls.py`, and a measured bypass curve for f.
- Next: source f (bypass flow of a sectional valve at partial lever), the only input the 3n result turns on.

## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 6d: paper section 3.5 gains 3l and 3n (a writing step, no experiment)
- Added the pump fuel-and-parts result (3l: +14.1% shaft energy at 1.2 x, break-even machine hour $7.67) and the surplus-flow fraction sweep (3n: break-even 36.1 to 18.8 bar as f falls from 1 to 0) to section 3.5, including that both predictions were refuted. 8 new rows in `paper/check_numbers.py`, 55 claims, 0 missing.
- Next: a sourced f (bypass-flow curve of a sectional valve); the compensator.

## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 3n: how much of 3m's pump-size penalty is the "full pump flow at load pressure" assumption? (prediction registered before running)
- Why: 3m's limit says open-centre is charged the whole pump flow at the highest load pressure in every work phase, which is the pessimistic end; a real spool throttles the centre gallery only partly. `experiments/surplus_fraction.py` reruns 3m with a surplus fraction f: in each work phase open-centre draws `q_dem + f (Q_pump - q_dem)` at the highest load pressure plus the spool drop (f = 1 is 3m, f = 0 is an optimistic demand-matched open-centre). Pump scales 1.0 and 1.2 x, f in 1, 0.75, 0.5, 0.25, 0. The idle neutral term and the stretched-time charge are as in 3m.
- **Prediction (before running):** the 1.2 x break-even dp_ls falls monotonically as f falls; at f = 0.5 it is below 3f's no-time-penalty 28.0 bar, and at f = 0 it is below 14 bar (the low end of the load-sensing margin range), because a demand-matched open-centre differs from LS only by the margin-versus-spool-drop gap plus idle. Prior 65%. Refuted if the 1.2 x break-even at f = 0.5 is 28 bar or more, or at f = 0 is 14 bar or more.
- Limits that carry over: assumed force-model demands; f is swept, not sourced; no compensator.

### Outcome of 3n (`experiments/surplus_fraction_results.txt`, seeded, 1,500 cycles per cell, 53 s)
- Break-even dp_ls (median LS/OC = 1) at 1.0 x pump: 36.1 bar (f = 1, reproduces 3m), 32.0, 27.5, 23.1, **18.8** (f = 0). At 1.2 x: 44.5, 38.1, **31.7** (f = 0.5), 25.2, **18.8** (f = 0). **The prediction is refuted on both bounds**: monotone in f held, but 31.7 bar at f = 0.5 is above 28, and 18.8 bar at f = 0 is above 14.
- Reading: pump size stops mattering at f = 0 (18.8 bar at both scales, as it must: no surplus flow is charged). Even a perfectly demand-matched open-centre (f = 0) only beats a load-sensing circuit once the LS margin exceeds about 19 bar, because open-centre pays a 6 bar spool drop against the margin plus LS standby flow at idle; so the 14 to 30 bar sourced margin range straddles the break-even whatever f is, and the answer turns on f between 0.25 and 0.75 for a 25 bar margin. The 3m pump-size effect (break-even 36 to 45 bar) is a feature of f = 1 and shrinks by 13 bar at f = 0.5.
- Limits: f is a swept parameter, not measured; one f for all phases; demands from the assumed force model; no compensator. Not a measurement of any machine.
- Next: measure or source f (a bypass-flow curve for a sectional valve at partial lever); the compensator.

## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 3m: what does the larger pump cost in energy, and where does the break-even dp_ls go? (prediction registered before running)
- Why: 3k's "Next" (a sibling run's 3l priced the same pump in fuel and parts). A pump 20% above peak demand removes open-centre's median time penalty, but in this model open-centre draws full pump flow at the highest load pressure in every phase, so a bigger pump should cost shaft energy. `experiments/pump_size_energy.py` reruns 3i (rated-sizing spools at 1.0 x rated, load factor 0.346, LS standby 2 L/min, open-centre charged full pump power for stretched seconds) with the open-centre pump scaled 1.0, 1.1, 1.2 and 1.5 x and the neutral drop read from the PVG 32 curve at the scaled flow; load-sensing is unchanged. Break-even dp_ls = the LS margin at which median LS/OC = 1.
- **Prediction (before running):** break-even dp_ls rises monotonically with pump size (more open-centre energy), so it is above 3i's 36.1 bar at 1.2 x. Prior 80%. Refuted if the 1.2 x break-even is at or below 36.1 bar.
- Limits that carry over: demands from the assumed force model; work-port-to-tank drop as rating point; no compensator; parts cost of the larger pump not priced here.

### Outcome of 3m (`experiments/pump_size_energy_results.txt`, seeded, 1,500 cycles per cell, 23 s)
- Break-even dp_ls (median LS/OC = 1): **36.1 bar** at 1.0 x (reproduces 3i), **40.0** at 1.1 x, **44.5** at 1.2 x, and **no crossing up to 60 bar** at 1.5 x (LS/OC 0.62 at 26 bar). Median open-centre time penalty 6.25%, 0.93%, 0.00%, 0.00%. **Prediction held**: monotone rise, 44.5 > 36.1.
- Reading: removing open-centre's starvation penalty with a bigger pump is not free in energy. Against the no-time-penalty break-even of 28.0 bar (2n/3f), the 6.25% penalty at a pump sized to demand raised it to 36.1 bar (open-centre burns extra seconds at full pump power); a 20% larger pump removes that penalty but its extra flow raises the break-even again, to 44.5 bar, so in energy terms the oversized pump is worse for open-centre than the starved one. A load-sensing margin of 14 to 30 bar beats open-centre on shaft energy at every pump size tested, by more as the pump grows. This does not price the extra pump or the productivity lost to starvation.
- Limits: open-centre is modelled as full pump flow at the highest load pressure (the pessimistic end, 2a), which is what makes a bigger pump cost energy; a real open-centre pump with a flow-sharing or unloading valve would not. Not a measurement.
- Next: the compensator; price the pump rungs against the parts cost of milestone 4.
## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 3l: what does the larger pump cost in fuel and parts against the time it saves? (prediction registered before running)
- Why: 3k's "Next". The open-centre time penalty vanishes with a pump 1.2 x the peak demand, but a larger fixed pump moves more neutral flow through the valve during idle (flow x p_n, 2i to 2n) and costs more. `experiments/pump_cost.py` reruns 3k's cycles at 0.8, 1.0, 1.2 and 1.5 x pump with the energy of `cycle_energy` on the stretched cycle plus the idle neutral flow at `Q x p_n` (p_n from `pvg_neutral`, load factor 0.346 as in 3i), then reports per scale: energy per cycle, cycle time (stretched work plus idle), and the implied break-even value of a machine hour, ((extra fuel + pump premium amortised over 2,000 h) per hour) / (hours saved per working hour). Pump price scales linearly with flow from the one listed gear-pump price in `payback.py` (ASSUMED, swept to 3 x that slope); diesel $6.28/gal as in `payback.py`.
- **Prediction (before running):** open-centre shaft energy per cycle rises with pump size by under 5% from 1.0 x to 1.2 x and under 15% from 1.0 x to 1.5 x, and the break-even machine-hour value at 1.2 x is below $50 per hour. Prior 50%. Refuted if either energy rise is above its bound or the break-even value is above $50/h.

### Outcome of 3l (`experiments/pump_cost_results.txt`, seeded, 1,500 cycles per row)
- Mean open-centre shaft energy per cycle (idle neutral flow included): 88.0 kJ at 1.0 x, **+14.1%** at 1.2 x, **+34.8%** at 1.5 x; mean cycle time -2.55% and -4.70%. **The prediction is refuted on energy** (I bounded the 1.0-to-1.2 rise at 5% and 1.0-to-1.5 at 15%). The second half held: the break-even value of a machine hour is **$7.67** (1.2 x) and **$10.27** (1.5 x) with pump price proportional to flow, $8.82 and $11.84 at three times that slope, far under the $50 bound.
- Reading: the energy rise is large because the fixed-pump model charges the whole pump flow at load pressure in every work phase, so a 20% larger pump burns about 20% more hydraulic power while moving, not only in neutral. Even so the fuel is cheap against a machine hour, so a 1.2 x pump pays whenever an hour of the machine is worth more than about $9. The pump premium is negligible beside the fuel (0.015 to 0.044 $/h).
- 0.8 x is not reported as a result: 1,357 of 1,500 cycles have a phase demanding more than the pump delivers and are dropped by `cycle_energy` (3k handled this with the pump-limit factor), so its row is for 143 unrepresentative cycles.
- Limits: the pump price is one retail listing scaled linearly (assumed); idle time is fixed from the unstretched cycle, so saved time is counted as hours saved, not idle energy; OC energy model has no relief or flow-compensated pump; diesel price from a secondary summary; load-sensing is not re-sized (it needs no larger pump in 3k).
- Next: the same break-even against a load-sensing pump (the real alternative to oversizing); add the compensator.

## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 3k: does the open-centre time penalty depend on how big the pump is? (prediction registered before running)
- Why: 3a to 3j fixed the pump at `pc.Q_PUMP` (45 L/min). The starvation that costs open-centre its time comes from the pump flow being shared by spool openings, so the pump size should matter, and a reader sizing a machine picks it. `experiments/pump_size.py` reruns 3h/3i's rated-sizing slowdown (smallest covering PVG 32 spool, rated drop read at 1.0 x rated, `data/pvg32_dp_at_rated.csv`) on the same random cycles with the pump flow scaled to 0.8, 1.0, 1.2 and 1.5 times, demands unchanged, and charges load-sensing its own pump-limit factor max(1, sum q / Q) (3c). Net penalty = open-centre cycle time over load-sensing cycle time.
- **Prediction (before running):** the net open-centre penalty is non-increasing in pump size (median at 1.5 x no higher than at 1.0 x, which reproduces 3h's 6.25% before the LS floor), and at 0.8 x it exceeds 6.25% only because the pump limit binds both circuits, so the net penalty at 0.8 x is smaller than the gross one. Prior 60%. Refuted if the net median at 1.5 x is above that at 1.0 x.
- Limits that carry over: demands from the assumed force model; work-port-to-tank drop as rating point; no compensator.

### Outcome of 3k (`experiments/pump_size_results.txt`, seeded, 1,500 cycles per row)
- Net open-centre penalty over load-sensing, median (p95): **17.51%** (21.62%) at 0.8 x pump, **6.25%** (21.62%) at 1.0 x, **0.00%** (21.62%) at 1.2 x, **0.00%** (10.68%) at 1.5 x. Gross open-centre penalty at 0.8 x is 22.27%, load-sensing's own pump-limit penalty there 3.80%. **Both parts of the prediction held.**
- Reading: the median penalty vanishes with a pump 20% larger than the peak demand needs, so 3h's 6.25% is a statement about a pump sized at the demand, not about open-centre as such; the tail (p95 21.6%) needs 1.5 x to fall. This is a cost, not a free fix: a larger pump costs more and carries more idle and neutral flow, which this step does not price.
- Next: price the larger pump (parts cost from milestone 4, neutral flow from 2i to 2n) against the time saved; add the compensator.

## 2026-10-31 · Milestone 3j: the manual's P-to-A flow curves pin the rating-point drop (not pre-registered)
- Why: 3h and 3i used the work-port-to-tank drop (p. 99) as the rating point and said the P-to-A flow curve was the missing check. The same Danfoss PVG 32 manual (BC152886483664en-001601, July 2024) plots flow against spool travel at 10 bar margin (p. 95) and 20 bar margin (p. 96), uncompensated, P to A. `experiments/digitise_pvg_flow.py` reads each spool's shaded band at full travel from a 300 dpi render (`experiments/data/pvg32_flow_full_travel.csv`); `experiments/pvg_flow_rating.py` turns it into an implied rating drop.
- Honesty note: I looked at the plots and ran a rough read before writing this entry, so no prediction is registered.
- Result (`experiments/pvg_flow_rating_results.txt`): full-travel flow at 10 bar is **1.14 times rated** (median; 0.84 to 1.23), so with an orifice law the rating applies at a P-to-A drop of **7.3 bar** (median, spools A to E; 6.6 to 8.2), the same median as 3h's 7.3 bar from p. 99 (per spool they differ by up to 5 bar: 11.8 against 7.3 for A, 5.3 against 8.2 for C). Median exponent between the two margins 0.53 (orifice 0.50); spool scatter 0.38 to 0.96.
- Reading: two independent figures give the same median rating drop, which removes 3h's main caveat about which drop the rating refers to, and puts the 3i sizing in the middle of the 0.8 to 1.2 times rated band (6.25% median time penalty, break-even 36.1 bar) instead of leaving it as one reading of three.
- Limits: the figure is "theoretical performance" and plots a band across first to eighth section, I use the band middle; the margin is the pump margin, not necessarily the drop across the P-to-A path alone; spool AA (5 L/min, read error about a third of its flow) is excluded from the A to E medians; the exponent scatter shows the curves are not one orifice law per spool.
- Next: use the P-to-A curve's spool-travel axis (not only full travel) for feathering; add the compensator.

## 2026-10-31 · Milestone 6c: paper section 3.5 gains 3i (a writing step, no experiment)
- Added the manual-curve sizing result (6.25% median penalty, break-even 36.1 bar, 34.0 to 38.9 across the rating-point reading) to section 3.5; 5 new rows in `paper/check_numbers.py`, all matching `time_penalised_rated_results.txt` (37 claims, 0 missing).
- Next: the manual's P-to-A flow curve; the compensator.

## 2026-10-31 · Milestone 3i: 3f's break-even with the manual-curve sizing (prediction registered before running)
- Why: 3h's "Next". `experiments/time_penalised_rated.py` is 3f with each phase stretched by the rated-sizing slowdown of 3h (spool size and drop at 1.0 x rated from `data/pvg32_dp_at_rated.csv`; 0.8 and 1.2 x rated as sensitivity) instead of the closing-law factor. Same cycles, same upper-bound charge (open-centre pump at full power for the extra seconds), load factor 0.346, standby 2 L/min.
- **Prediction (before running):** the break-even dp_ls at 1.0 x rated lies between 30 and 38 bar (3f's no-penalty value is 28.0 and its k = 1 value 35.8; 3h's 6.25% median penalty is milder than k = 1's 21.6%). Prior 65%. Refuted if it is below 29 or above 38 bar.

### Outcome of 3i (`experiments/time_penalised_rated_results.txt`, 10 s, seeded)
- Break-even dp_ls (bar): 28.0 (no penalty, reproduces 2n), **36.1** (rated sizing at 1.0 x rated, median time penalty 6.25%), 38.9 (0.8 x rated, 16.81%), 34.0 (1.2 x rated, 2.64%). **Prediction held** (30 to 38 bar).
- LS/OC at the Rexroth 14 bar margin: 0.757 without penalty, 0.663 with it (0.642 to 0.682 across the +-20% rating-point reading); at 26 bar 0.965 falls to 0.846. So at the thesis 25 bar margin load-sensing already wins once slowness is charged, where 2n had it losing.
- Reading: charged in energy, the 3h penalty moves the break-even from 28 to 34 to 39 bar, so 2n's ranking at the sourced 14 bar margin stands and strengthens. 3f's "credible range 28 to 29.5" came from a closing law the manual contradicts; with the manual's own spool curves the range is 34 to 39 bar.
- Limits: the charge is an upper bound (full pump power during the stretch); the 3h caveat (work-port-to-tank drop used as the rating point) carries over; no compensator or feathering; one load factor.
- Next: find the manual's P-to-A flow curve; add the compensator; fold 3i into the paper's section 3.5 and `check_numbers.py`.

## 2026-10-31 · Milestone 3h: 3g with the drop at rated flow read from the manual's own curves (prediction registered before running)
- Why: 3g swept dp_ref (5 to 35 bar) because the rated drop was not in the pages read. The same manual (BC152886483664en-001601, July 2024, p. 99, "Pressure drop at maximum spool travel position, A/B to T", opened this session) plots drop against flow for each spool. `experiments/digitise_pvg_dp.py` picks each curve's pixels by colour from a 300 dpi render and reads the drop at 0.8, 1.0 and 1.2 times rated flow (`experiments/data/pvg32_dp_at_rated.csv`); `experiments/rated_sizing_curve.py` sizes each spool by its own value.
- Read values at rated flow (bar): A 11.8, B 6.7, C 5.3, D 7.3, E 7.6. Caveat that matters: the figure is the **work-port-to-tank** drop at maximum travel, not the P-to-A drop the flow rating may refer to; I use it as the best available measure of each spool's size, not as proof of the rating point. Read error about 1 bar.
- **Prediction (before running):** the values sit in 5 to 12 bar, the band where 3g's penalty fell from 15.8% (5 bar) to 0.9% (10 bar), so I predict a median time penalty between 1% and 8% and a dig-phase stall-bound share between 10% and 40%.

### Outcome of 3h (`experiments/rated_sizing_curve_results.txt`, 2 s, seeded)
- dp at 1.0 x rated: median time penalty **6.25%** (p95 21.6%), dig-phase stall-bound share **35.5%**. Both inside the predicted bands (1 to 8%, 10 to 40%).
- Sensitivity to the flow at which the rating is read: 0.8 x rated gives 16.8% / 47.5%; 1.2 x rated gives 2.6% / 25.4%. So the penalty is 2.6 to 16.8% across a +-20% reading of the rating point.
- Reading: 3g's comfortable case (dp_ref of 14 bar or more, zero penalty) is not what the manual's curves show: they give 5 to 12 bar, so with real discrete spools open-centre sections starve light actuators in a third of multi-actuator dig phases. 3f's time-penalised break-even (28 to 29.5 bar, no k) is therefore an under-statement of load-sensing's advantage if the penalty is charged as energy; it has not been rerun with this sizing.
- Limits: the figure is the work-port-to-tank drop, not necessarily the rating point; read error about 1 bar; no compensator or feathering; demands from the assumed force model; sizing rule (smallest covering spool) is mine.
- Next: rerun 3f's break-even with this sizing; find the manual's P-to-A flow curve; add the compensator.

It fails if the rated drop is as low as 5 to 10 bar, where discrete spool sizes starve light actuators. The result hinges on dp_ref, which still needs the manual's flow-curve axis (not read).

No number is new; each is copied from `flow_share_shape_results.txt`, `flow_share_ls_results.txt` or `time_penalised_results.txt`, and 10 new rows in `paper/check_numbers.py` hold them (32 claims, 0 missing).

## 2026-10-30 · Milestone 3f: does the open-centre time penalty move 2n's load-sensing break-even? (not pre-registered)
- Why: 2n ranked on energy alone and 3a to 3e priced the time penalty separately. `experiments/time_penalised.py` stretches each open-centre phase by `phase_slowdown` (k = 1 to 10, p_n 10.91 bar) and charges the open-centre pump full power for the extra seconds, an upper bound on the energy cost of slowness. Load-sensing is charged nothing extra (3c). Load factor 0.346, standby 2 L/min, 1,500 cycles per cell.
- Honesty note: I ran the script once before writing a prediction here, so no prediction is registered for this step. What I would have predicted was "break-even stays at 28 bar for k >= 3 where 3d's penalty is zero".

### Outcome of 3f (`experiments/time_penalised_results.txt`, 26 s, seeded)
- Break-even dp_ls (bar): 28.0 (no penalty, reproduces 2n), **35.8** (k 1), 31.1 (k 1.5), 29.4 (k 2), 28.3 (k 3), 28.0 (k 5), **29.5 (k 10)**. At the Rexroth 14 bar margin LS/OC is 0.67 to 0.76 for every k; the thesis 25 bar margin favours LS at k <= 2 and k = 10 too.
- The "28.0 for k >= 3" guess held at k = 3 and 5 but **k = 10 is not monotone**: the median time penalty is 0 yet the break-even moves 1.5 bar, because the p95 tail (6.7% in 3d) is enough to change the cycle-mean energy ratio. Read k = 10 with 3e's validity limit in mind.
- Reading: charging slowness in energy can only help load-sensing, and it widens the margin by 0 to 8 bar depending on how fast the bypass closes. 2n's ranking at both sourced margins therefore stands, and gets stronger if the bypass closes slowly. The 35.8 bar is for the k the PVG 32 manual contradicts (3d), so the credible range is 28 to 29.5.
- Limits: the stretch charge is an upper bound (full pump power); no feathering; one load factor; k a one-number family.
- Next: size sections from the PVG 32 rated flows A to E (removes k), then the compensator.

## 2026-10-30 · Milestone 3e: was 3d's k = 20 row only a bracket bug? (planned before running)
- Why: 3d put the bad k = 20 row at 10.9 bar down to `section_area` bracketing the supply pressure at p_i + 400 bar. Fixed the bracket (it now grows until the sign changes; 1 new test) and reran `experiments/flow_share_shape.py` with k up to 50.
- **Prediction (before running):** after the fix the median penalty is 0% for k = 20 at p_n 10.9 bar (prior 60%). Refuted if it is above 1%.

### Outcome of 3e (`experiments/flow_share_shape_results.txt`, 5 s, seeded)
- k <= 10 rows are unchanged to two decimals (the old bracket only failed above that). **Prediction refuted:** at p_n 10.9 bar k = 20 gives a median penalty of 17.1% (stall share 35.9%) and k = 50 gives 21.6% (100%); at p_n 1.44 bar k = 50 gives 11.8%.
- Reading: the remaining rise is not a solver bug. With k this large each section is sized for a supply pressure above the 250 bar relief setting (its area is about 0.6 to 1.3, against 2 to 12 at k = 1), so with all sections open the pump flow cannot pass below relief and the lighter actuators are starved. That is a model-validity limit: the sizing rule "passes its demand alone at the supply pressure the closing law implies" stops describing a real valve when that pressure exceeds relief. So 3d's usable range is k up to about 10 at 10.9 bar and up to about 20 at 1.44 bar; the conclusion that a bypass closing inside the dead band (k >= 3 to 5) makes the open-centre time penalty small stands for k in range, and rows beyond are outside the model.
- Limits: validity range read off the results, not enforced in code; sizing by rated flows A to E from the manual (3d "Next") would remove the issue and is still not done.
- Next: size sections from the PVG 32 rated flows, add the compensator and feathering, then the time-penalised energy ranking.

## 2026-10-30 · Milestone 3d: what does the PVG 32 manual say about how fast the bypass closes? (planned before running)
- Why: 3b's "Next" was to fit k from a bypass-versus-stroke curve. Opened this session (assets.danfoss.com now resolves): Danfoss PVG 32 Technical Information BC152886483664en-001601, July 2024 (the 2n source was the May 2021 revision), p. 95: "In total neutral position the connection to tank is less than 10 % of the full flow function. But when moving the spool out of neutral position but still within dead band area the connection will close." The same page's flow-versus-stroke curves show zero work-port flow for roughly the first 1.5 to 2 mm of a 7 mm stroke. The manual has **no** bypass-area-versus-stroke curve, so k cannot be fitted; it gives a bound: the bypass is shut before any section opens, which in 3a's terms is a large k.
- Plan: add k = 5, 10, 20 to `experiments/flow_share_shape.py` (same cycles as 3b).
- **Prediction (before running):** at p_n 10.9 bar the median penalty is 0% and the dig-phase stall share is under 2% for k >= 3 (prior 75%). Refuted if the median is above 1% at k = 5 or 10.

### Outcome of 3d (`experiments/flow_share_shape_results.txt`, 5 s, seeded)
- p_n 10.9 bar: median penalty 0.00% for k = 1.5 to 10; p95 0.07% (k 5), 6.69% (k 10); stall share 0.0% for k >= 3. Prediction held for k = 5 and 10.
- p_n 1.44 bar: median 0% from k = 5, p95 21.6% (k 5), 1.68% (k 10), 0.00% (k 20).
- **k = 20 at p_n 10.9 is a solver artefact, not physics:** median 8.66%, non-monotone. `section_area` brackets the supply pressure at p_i + 400 bar, above the 250 bar relief; with a huge k the sizing root lies outside it. Do not read k > 10 rows at 10.9 bar.
- Reading: if the bypass closes inside the dead band, as the manual states, 3a's open-centre cycle-time penalty is small for the PVG 32 neutral drop (median 0, p95 under 7%), and 3a's "6.4%" was a product of k = 1, a closing law the manual contradicts. At the 1.44 bar catalogue-valve drop the penalty survives to k of about 3 to 5. So the cost of open-centre in time is not established; the energy ranking of 2n stands without a time penalty for this valve.
- Limits: "closes in the dead band" is a qualitative statement read from a manual page, not a curve; k is still a one-number family; the factor-2 stall bound and the sizing-alone assumption remain; the section-sizing solver needs a wider bracket before k > 10 is usable (not fixed here).
- Next: fix the bracket (or size sections by the manual's rated flows A to E), add the compensator and feathering, then a time-penalised energy ranking.

## 2026-10-30 · Milestone 3c: how much of 3a's penalty is open-centre, and how much is just the pump limit? (planned before running)
- Why: 3a and 3b set load-sensing's cycle-time factor to 1.0 by construction. But a compensated valve also slows when the actuators' demands add to more than the 45 L/min pump flow. `experiments/flow_share_ls.py` charges LS `max(1, sum q / Q_pump)` per phase, floors open-centre at that, and reports open-centre cycle time over load-sensing cycle time. The PVG 32 bypass-versus-stroke data for fitting k is not reachable this session (Danfoss document URLs return 404/403), so k stays 1.
- **Prediction (before running):** load-sensing's own median penalty is under 1% (most demands fit in the pump flow), and the net open-centre median at p_n 10.9 bar is within 1 point of 3a's 6.4% (prior 65%). Refuted if the LS median is 1% or more, or the net median differs from 6.4% by more than 1 point.

### Outcome of 3c (`experiments/flow_share_ls.py`, `flow_share_ls_results.txt`, 2 s, seeded; 1 new test)
- Load-sensing's own pump-limit penalty is **0.00%** (median and 95th percentile) at both neutral drops; across 5 x 300 sampled cycles no phase's summed demand exceeds the 45 L/min pump, so no phase was floored. Net open-centre over load-sensing: p_n 10.9 bar median **5.80%**, p95 21.62%; p_n 1.44 bar median 21.62%. **Prediction held** (LS median under 1%; 5.80% is within 1 point of 3a's 6.4%; the 5.80 is 3b's k = 1 row, 300 cycles per seed instead of 1,000).
- Reading: 3a's "one-sided comparison" caveat is closed for the sampled cycles: the penalty is entirely the uncompensated open-centre valve's flow sharing, none of it a pump limit both circuits share. This holds only for the duty-cycle generator's demands (2d); a larger excavator task could exceed the pump.
- Limits: unchanged from 3a/3b (k = 1, factor-2 stall bound, no feathering, no dynamics). k still needs the PVG 32 bypass-versus-stroke curve, not reachable this session.

## 2026-10-30 · Milestone 3b: is 3a's cycle-time penalty an artefact of the bypass closing law? (planned before running)
- Why: 3a listed "one bypass shape (area closes linearly with section area)" as a limit. `flow_share.py` now takes k, the bypass area closed per unit of section area (k = 1 is 3a); `experiments/flow_share_shape.py` sweeps k = 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3 at both sourced neutral drops, 5 seeds x 300 cycles (3a used 1,000 per seed, so the k = 1 row differs slightly from 3a).
- **Prediction (before running):** the 3a median (6.4%) moves by more than a factor of 2 across k = 0.5 to 2: prior 70%. Refuted if it stays within 3.2% to 12.8%.

### Outcome of 3b (`experiments/flow_share_shape_results.txt`, 3 s, seeded; 1 new test, all pass)
- p_n 10.9 bar: median penalty 21.6% (k 0.5), 5.8% (k 1), **0.0%** (k 1.5, 2, 3); p95 21.6, 21.6, 21.6, 9.6, 1.9. Dig phases at the stall bound: 82.9, 34.7, 10.3, 1.1, 0.0%. Prediction held.
- p_n 1.44 bar: median 21.6% for every k up to 2 and 20.0% at k = 3: the stall bound is hit in 49 to 100% of dig phases whatever the closing law.
- Reading: 3a's "median 6.4%" is a statement about one assumed closing law, not about open-centre valves. What is robust is the sign (never faster than load-sensing) and that a low neutral drop (1.44 bar) leaves little pressure rise to unstall the heavy actuator. How fast a real spool closes its bypass is a datasheet quantity (the PVG 32 curve behind 2n has it) and is the next thing to source.
- Limits: k is a single-number family, not a measured area curve; the factor-2 stall bound is still an assumption; no dynamics.
- Next: fit k from the PVG 32 bypass-versus-spool-stroke data behind 2n, then rerun 3a with it.

## 2026-10-30 · Milestone 3a: how much longer does an open-centre parallel valve make the dig phase? (planned before running)
- Why: milestone 3 (dynamics) was never started and every ranking so far is energy only; cycle time is the other half of the question. Full dynamics (compensator, line volumes) need component data I do not have, so this step is the steady-state part: `src/heavy_hydraulics/flow_share.py`, orifice sections sized to pass their demand alone, a bypass that closes as sections open, p_n 10.9 bar (2n) and 1.44 bar (2i), 5 x 1,000 random cycles of 2d. Only the dig phase has two actuators. No operator feathering (so an upper-ish bound before a person corrects it); a stalled actuator (supply pressure below its load) is charged the bound factor 2 (sequential motion).
- **Prediction (before running):** median cycle-time penalty under 2% but 95th percentile 10% or more (prior 55%): most cycles lose little because the closing bypass lifts supply pressure, a tail where the high-pressure actuator stalls. Refuted if the median is 2% or more, or the 95th percentile is under 10%.
- Limits known in advance: orifice law, one bypass shape (area closes linearly with section area), sections sized alone, no compensator dynamics, no line volume: this is not yet the dynamic model.

### Outcome of 3a (`experiments/flow_share.py`, `flow_share_results.txt`, about 1 minute, seeded; 3 new tests, 10 pass)
- p_n 10.9 bar: cycle-time penalty median **6.4%**, 95th percentile 21.6% (the cap); 65.5% of cycles slow at all; 35.6% of dig phases sit at the stall bound (factor 2). p_n 1.44 bar: 21.6% median, 98.4% of dig phases at the bound.
- **Prediction half refuted:** median 6.4% is not under 2% (the 95th percentile of at least 10% held). The closing bypass did not save most cycles.
- Reading: with no operator feathering, an uncompensated open-centre valve loses real cycle time in the dig phase because the lighter actuator takes the flow, and a freer centre (smaller p_n) makes it worse, not better. That is a cost the energy-only rankings (2f to 2o) leave out, and it works against open-centre exactly where 2i to 2k had it winning on energy. Load-sensing is 1.0 here by construction, so the comparison is one-sided until a compensator model exists.
- Limits: the 21.6% cap and the 98% stall share are mostly the bound I set (factor 2), not a measurement; the result depends on sizing each section alone and on the linear bypass closure; real operators feather. Treat it as a direction (open-centre pays in time, not only in energy), not a number. Next: a dynamic model with pressure compensators and a feathering rule, then a time-penalised energy ranking.

## 2026-10-30 · Milestone 2o: how far does the unsourced LS standby flow move 2n's break-even? (planned before running)
- Why: 2m and 2n both end with "a standby flow from a pump datasheet". Hosts for datasheets are still unreachable, so sweep it instead: `experiments/standby_sweep.py`, p_n 10.91 bar (2n), load factor 0.346, q_sb 0, 1, 2, 4, 6, 8, 10 L/min, dp_ls 10 to 40 bar.
- **Prediction (before running):** break-even falls monotonically with q_sb, reproduces 28.0 bar at 2 L/min, is above 30 bar at 0 and below 25 bar by 8 L/min (prior 70%), so at q_sb of 6 L/min or more the thesis 25 bar margin loses again. Refuted if non-monotone, or if the 8 L/min break-even is 25 bar or more.

### Outcome of 2o (`experiments/standby_sweep.py`, `standby_sweep_results.txt`, about 1 minute, seeded)
- Break-even dp_ls (bar) by standby flow: **29.1** (0 L/min), 28.5 (1), 28.0 (2, reproduces 2n), 26.9 (4), 25.9 (6), **25.0** (8), 24.0 (10). Monotone; about 0.5 bar of margin lost per L/min.
- **Prediction refuted on both numeric lines.** At 0 L/min the break-even is 29.1, not above 30; at 8 L/min it is 25.0 (not under 25). Direction and the 2 L/min reproduction held.
- Reading: the standby flow, unsourced, is a weak lever: across 0 to 10 L/min the break-even stays within 24 to 29 bar, so the thesis 25 bar margin sits on the boundary only for 8 L/min or more, and the Rexroth 14 bar margin wins at every standby flow tested (LS/OC 0.75 to 0.80). 2n's ranking (load-sensing ahead at both sourced margins) holds unless the pump's standby flow is about 8 L/min or more, which is large for a 45 L/min pump.
- Limits: same as 2n (one valve curve reading, one load factor); the standby flow is swept, not sourced.
- Next: a sourced standby flow would only narrow a 5 bar band; larger remaining gaps are the dynamic model (milestone 3) and a measured trace (H4).

## 2026-10-30 · Milestone 2n: with the open-centre neutral drop read from Danfoss's PVG 32 curve, who wins at idle? (planned before running)
- Why: every ranking since 2i hinged on p_n = 1.44 bar from one Salami valve catalogue (a 75 L/min valve, no input module). This session the Danfoss host is reachable. The PVG 32 Technical Information (BC152886483664, rev. May 2021; PDF opened, p. 16) has a curve "pressure drop, bypass in neutral" for its open-centre PVP input module: about 8.5 bar at near-zero flow rising linearly to about 16 bar at 140 L/min (read off the figure, +-0.5 bar). PVG 32 is a mobile/excavator-class proportional valve, rated 140 L/min. At the model's 45 L/min pump, p_n = 8.5 + 7.5 x 45/140 = 10.9 bar. The same manual has no LS margin or standby flow I could find (searched for margin, standby, bypass in the German text); the pump's are still 2j's.
- Plan (`experiments/pvg_neutral.py`, `ratio` from 2g, load factor 0.346, LS standby 2 L/min assumed, 600 cycles x 5 seeds): p_n = 10.9 bar (and the +-0.5 bar reading band at both ends: 10.4 and 11.4), dp_ls 10, 14, 20, 25, 28 bar, and the break-even dp_ls by interpolation on a 2 bar grid.
- **Prediction (before running):** the break-even dp_ls is about 28 bar (2l's line 19.3 + 0.92 (p_n - 1.44) gives 28.0; prior 70%), so load-sensing wins at 14, 20 and 25 bar, both sourced margins, reversing 2i for this valve. Refuted if the break-even is under 25 or over 31 bar, or if LS/OC at dp_ls 25 is 1.0 or more.
- Limits known in advance: the drop is the PVP module's bypass curve (sections add more in neutral, so p_n here is if anything low); a figure reading; a Danfoss valve paired with a Rexroth LS margin; one load factor; standby flow assumed.

### Outcome of 2n (`experiments/pvg_neutral.py`, 8 s, seeded; `pvg_neutral_results.txt`)
- p_n at 45 L/min is 10.91 bar. Break-even dp_ls **28.0** bar (27.5 to 28.4 across the +-0.5 bar reading band); LS/OC median is monotone in dp_ls.
- **Prediction held** (28.0 against "about 28", inside 25 to 31; LS/OC at 25 bar is 0.949, under 1.0). LS/OC median at dp_ls 14: 0.759 (LS worse in 0.8% of cycles), 20: 0.863 (11.8%), 25: 0.949 (33.6%).
- Reading: on a PVG 32-class excavator valve's own bypass curve, load-sensing uses less shaft energy than open-centre at the measured load factor for BOTH sourced margins (Rexroth 14 bar, thesis 25 bar), up to a margin of 28 bar. This reverses 2i (which used a 1.4 bar neutral drop from a 75 L/min catalogue valve) and 2l's "no further simulation resolves the sign": the neutral drop that matters for an excavator-class valve is about 11 bar, not 1.4. 2f's headline (load-sensing saves energy at the measured load factor) is back, now with a sourced p_n. The open-centre neutral drop of a valve with sections adds to this, so 10.9 bar is if anything low.
- Limits: as planned. A figure reading (+-0.5 bar), one manufacturer, the LS margin and standby flow are not from the same machine, the Salami and Danfoss values differ by 9.5 bar because the Danfoss curve is for the input module's bypass (relief/bypass path) and I have not shown which one a given excavator's neutral path matches.

## 2026-10-30 · Milestone 2m: how far does the break-even LS margin move with load factor? (planned before running)
- Why: 2l's "Next": it is one load factor (0.346, the measured one). A reader's machine idles more or less than that. `experiments/ranking_map_lf.py`, 2l's model unchanged, load factor 0.15, 0.25, 0.346, 0.5, 0.7, p_n 1.44 and 6.44 bar, dp_ls 4 to 60 bar.
- **Prediction (before running):** the break-even falls monotonically as load factor rises (less idle time means less for load-sensing's low idle power to win back), from above 30 bar at 0.15 to under 20 bar at 0.7 at p_n 1.44, and at 0.346 it reproduces 2l's 19.3 (prior 80%). Refuted if non-monotone in load factor, or if the 0.7 break-even is 20 bar or more.

### Outcome of 2m (`experiments/ranking_map_lf.py`, 20 s, seeded; `ranking_map_lf_results.txt`)
- Break-even dp_ls (bar) at p_n 1.44: **12.9** (load factor 0.15), 16.8 (0.25), 19.3 (0.346, reproduces 2l), 21.7 (0.5), 22.9 (0.7). At p_n 6.44: 25.5, 24.5, 23.9, 23.3, 23.1. Monotone in dp_ls in every row.
- **Prediction refuted on direction.** I expected the break-even to fall as load factor rises. It rises at p_n 1.44 (12.9 to 22.9): with a near-free open-centre neutral (1.44 bar), idle time favours open-centre because load-sensing pays dp_ls on its standby flow, so the more a machine idles, the lower the LS margin load-sensing needs to win. Only when the open-centre neutral costs about 6 bar does idle favour load-sensing (break-even falls slightly, 25.5 to 23.1). The 0.346 row reproduces 2l (19.3, 23.9) and the 0.7 value is 22.9, over my 20 bar refutation line, so both stated refutation conditions fired.
- Reading: the sign of the circuit gap depends on load factor only when p_n is small. For the Rexroth 14 bar margin, load-sensing wins at every load factor tested with p_n 1.44 except 0.15 (14 is above 12.9); for the thesis 25 bar it loses at every load factor at p_n 1.44, and at 6.44 wins only at 0.15 (25 against 25.5, inside interpolation error). The measured margin and neutral pressure still decide it; load factor does not rescue either side.
- Limits: standby flow 2 L/min assumed (it scales the idle effect directly); one valve drop source; break-even by interpolation on a 4 bar grid.
- Next: a standby flow from a pump datasheet, since it multiplies the idle term that this run shows drives the load-factor dependence.

## 2026-10-29 · Milestone 2i: with the open-centre neutral pressure read from a published valve curve, who wins at idle? (planned before running)
- Why: the log's standing "Next" (since 2h): the ranking reduces to one number, p_n, and no source was found. This session found one: the Salami VD8A sectional-valve catalogue (PDF from psifluidpower.ca, opened and read; E0.06.0911.02.02), nominal 75 L/min, text "approximatively 1 bar for each spool at nominal flow" with spools in neutral, and a P-to-T curve per spool count (p. 7) that reads about 12.7 bar at 90 L/min for 8 spools (read off the figure, +-0.5 bar). Drop scales as Q^2. At the model's 45 L/min pump that is about 0.36 bar per section by the text, 0.4 by the curve.
- Plan (`experiments/neutral_sourced.py`, 2g's `ratio`, load factor 0.346, LS standby 2 L/min): p_n = valve drop for 3, 4, 6, 8 sections under both readings (text, curve), plus an ASSUMED extra 0, 2 or 5 bar for return line, filter and cooler (not in a valve catalogue). Report LS/OC median (5 to 95%) and the share of cycles where LS loses.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) valve-only (extra 0) LS/OC is above 1.05 for every section count up to 8, both readings (prior 85%): p_n* in 2h was 6.4 to 10.8 bar and the valve alone is under 4 bar. (2) With extra 5 bar and 4 sections or fewer, LS/OC stays above 1.0 (prior 80%). (3) The text and curve readings give p_n within 1 bar of each other at 4 sections (prior 70%). Refuted: (1) any cell at 1.05 or under; (2) any such cell below 1.0; (3) gap of 1 bar or more.
- Limits known in advance: one catalogue, one manufacturer's valve, a 75 L/min class part rather than an excavator block; a figure reading; excavators add boom-holding, anti-cavitation and flow-share elements, and many open-centre blocks have a parallel-series path with more drop; the extra bar is assumed; the LS side (standby, dp_ls 25 bar) is still unsourced.

### Outcome of 2i (`experiments/neutral_sourced.py`, about 1 minute, seeded; `experiments/neutral_sourced_results.txt`; 3,000 cycles per cell)
- Valve drop at 45 L/min: 1.1 to 1.2 bar (3 sections), 1.4 to 1.6 (4), 2.2 to 2.4 (6), 2.9 to 3.2 (8); the text and curve readings differ by under 0.4 bar everywhere.
- **(1) held:** valve-only LS/OC median is 1.081 to 1.122 in every cell, i.e. load-sensing uses 8 to 12% MORE shaft energy than open-centre at the measured load factor; LS is the worse circuit in 72 to 80% of cycles.
- **(2) held:** with an assumed extra 5 bar and 3 or 4 sections the ratio is 1.019 to 1.027, still above 1.
- **(3) held:** the readings differ by 0.15 bar at 4 sections.
- Reading: on this valve family the open-centre neutral drop is a few bar, below the 6 to 11 bar break-even of 2h, so 2f's headline "load-sensing saves 21% at the measured load factor" does not survive a sourced p_n; it flips to open-centre ahead by about 10% unless something else in the neutral path (return filter, cooler, a high-drop excavator block, a relief-limited bypass) adds 6 to 8 bar. Together with 4 (payback not met) this makes the sourced case against load-sensing on a small excavator, for energy, stronger. It remains a single manufacturer's 75 L/min-class valve; the LS side (dp_ls 25 bar, standby flow) is still unsourced and is the next input.
- Limits: as planned. The 8-spool figure value is a reading, the extra bar is assumed, and 2h's finding stands that whichever way the ranking falls it is set by p_n and dp_ls.
- Next: source dp_ls (LS margin pressure; pump datasheet) and an excavator-class open-centre block drop; regenerate the 2g table and the paper's ranking table with the sourced p_n.

- Prediction written before running: at the central point the cap never binds (uncapped = capped); at the upper corner (600 kg, 1.2 m, 0.92) it binds in most cycles and the uncapped share is above 10%. Refuted if the central cap binds in more than 5% of cycles.
- Outcome (5,000 cycles, same force model, sourced margins): central point (300 kg, 0.8 m, 0.7) uncapped 3.33% (open-centre) and 3.21% (load-sensing), identical to 5a's, cap binds in 0.0% of cycles. Lowest corner and (150 kg, 1.2 m, 0.92) also never bind (0.5%, 3.9%). Upper corner: uncapped 11.8% / 11.4% against 5a's 10.0% / 10.3%; the cap binds in 75% of cycles (26% at eta_rt 0.7, where uncapped is 9.0% / 8.7%). Both predictions held.
- Reading: 5a's central 3% needs no cap, so the doubt about the force model's non-physical lift phase (5.6x m g dh) does not touch it; only the quoted upper-corner 10% was cap-limited, and the uncapped figure is about 12%. Recovery stays a 1 to 12% lever on working-time shaft energy for swept, unsourced arm mass and drop height. Still no idle time, dynamics, accumulator sizing or price.
- Next: source an arm mass, price an accumulator or e-machine, then the paper draft (milestone 6).

## 2026-10-29 · Milestone 5a: how much of a cycle's pump-shaft energy can boom-lowering recovery return, and does it beat the circuit choice? (planned before running)
- Why: milestone 5 (energy recovery) is the last unbuilt rung before the paper, and 4 found fuel does not repay load-sensing. Is recovery a bigger lever than the circuit? `experiments/regen_bound.py` (stdlib, seconds). Per force-model cycle (`physics_cycle.one`, 20,000 cycles) the recoverable energy is E = (m_arm + m_load) g dh, with m_load the model's bucket-plus-soil mass, m_arm (boom, stick, bucket, effective at the boom cylinder) swept 150 to 600 kg and dh swept 0.5 to 1.2 m. **Neither m_arm nor dh is sourced** (component masses are not published for this class; swept, not chosen). Round-trip efficiency eta_rt is swept 0.3 to 0.92; the literature anchors found by search only (abstracts, not opened): 67.6% potential-energy reuse (dual-power boom, electric excavator, theory and experiment), 86% (digital pump with accumulator, simulation), up to 92% (1.6 t electro-hydraulic, simulation), 44.6% whole-cycle saving (50 t hybrid prototype, experiment). The saving is capped by the lift phase's shaft energy under each circuit (what the recovered energy can displace). Reported as a share of the cycle's open-centre and load-sensing shaft energy (no idle time added, so an upper bound on the share over working hours), next to the LS/OC gap.
- Consistency check I also run: the model's lift phase draws pressure x flow x time of useful work, which should be about m g dh for a real lift; the ratio of the model's lift useful energy to m g dh says whether the force model's lift phase is physical.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) at the central point (m_arm 300 kg, dh 0.8 m, eta_rt 0.7) the recovered share of cycle shaft energy is under 5% at the median, in both circuits. (2) Even at the upper corner (600 kg, 1.2 m, 0.92) the median share stays under 10%, so recovery tops out well below the 21% LS-over-OC gap at the measured load factor (2f). (3) The model's lift useful energy exceeds m g dh by at least 3x in the median cycle, i.e. the force model overstates lift work, which means its lift pressures are not purely gravitational. Refuted: (1) 5% or more; (2) 10% or more; (3) ratio under 3.
- Limits known in advance: potential energy of the arm only (no swing-brake or cylinder regen); m_arm and dh are swept; the recovery is not simulated dynamically (no accumulator sizing, no 3 s harvest-window limit that a review notes); cycles carry no idle time.

### Outcome of 5a (`experiments/regen_bound.py`, about 10 s; `regen_bound_results.txt`; 5,000 force-model cycles, sourced margins)
- Median recovered share of cycle pump-shaft energy (open-centre / load-sensing), capped by lift-phase shaft energy: central point (300 kg, 0.8 m, eta_rt 0.7) **3.3% / 3.2%**; lowest corner (150 kg, 0.5 m, 0.3) 0.5%; upper corner (600 kg, 1.2 m, 0.92) **10.0% / 10.3%**. The two circuits differ by under 0.3 points, so the choice of circuit barely changes what recovery can return.
- **(1) held** (3.3% and 3.2%, under 5%). **(2) refuted, narrowly:** the upper corner is 10.01% (open-centre) and 10.26% (load-sensing), not under 10%. **(3) held:** the force model's lift useful work is a median 5.6 times m_load g (0.8 m) (5 to 95%: 2.9 to 10.5), so its lift phase is not gravity alone: it also carries a pressure floor (`idle_u`) and the arm's own weight, which I did not separate.
- Reading: with unsourced arm mass and drop height swept over a 4x and 2.4x range, boom-lowering recovery is worth roughly 1 to 10% of cycle shaft energy at working time, 3% centrally, even at a 92% round trip. That is the same order as the load-sensing margin effect, and unlike load-sensing it costs an accumulator or an electric machine that this programme has not priced. I did NOT compare it with the 21% idle-time LS-over-OC gap of 2f: this run adds no idle time, and on a no-idle cycle LS/OC is 1.039 (LS loses, as 2d found), so the planned comparison to 21% was not like-for-like. Idle time lowers every share here further.
- Limits: m_arm, dh, eta_rt swept not sourced (the recovery efficiencies are from search abstracts, not opened papers); no dynamics, accumulator sizing or harvest-window limit; the cap uses the lift phase only; lift phase work is not physical (3), so the cap may be generous.
- Next: separate the lift phase into gravity and friction (so the cap is physical), price an accumulator or e-machine (milestone 4 style), and source a boom/arm mass.
